#### Key lessons learned from past GGW projects/programmes
83. Regional projects, programmes and programmatic approaches offer many lessons learned. The GGW GCF Regional Programme will build on these lessons – particularly those from the World Bank SAWAP and the GEF7 Impact Program on Food Resilience - to maximize the overall impacts of individual projects.

#### Table 13. Lessons learned and recommendations from regional approaches to programmes
| Lessons learned | Recommendations |
|-----------------|-----------------|
| Limiting the number of implementing agencies of regional support programmes helps reduce the complexity of reporting and bureaucracy. | It is key to ensure enough resources for knowledge management and M&E, both financial and human resources in support units and at the country level. |
| When building M&E and knowledge management platforms, their sustainability and maintenance (hosted vs. managed by service provider) should be carefully considered. | When working across a region with various languages, there is a need to allocate sufficient resources for the translation of documents. |
| Consistency of indicators across projects will allow for comparing/sharing. | A consultative committee for regional multi-agency programmes is preferred to a steering committee to reduce the complexity of governance arrangements. |
| A common ground for defining knowledge management activities (languages, sub-region, thematic and size of projects, etc.) has proved to increase impact. | Countries are interested in designing an M&E system to harmonize indicators and methodologies and create a community of practice133. |
| All projects should include geo-referencing, mapping and baseline data collection for all intervention sites134. | |

#### Addressing climate change
84. To address climate change impacts, IGREENFIN I and the Regional Support Programme will use an approach that builds on the countries’ climate adaptation and mitigation investment plans and on green financing models prepared for each country and compiled in Annex 2 - EFA. The green business projects to be supported by this programme are in line with the countries’ NDCs on the reduction of GHG emissions and vulnerabilities to the effects of climate change (see Table 14 below).

The selected West African countries have ratified the UN Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD), the Convention to Combat Desertification (CCD), the Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the Kyoto Protocol and are part of the Great Green Wall initiative (Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana are not members of the GGWI). As IFAD has more than 40 years experiences working in countries affected by natural disasters, including all the countries of the GGW. It will leverage this experience to implement IGREENFIN I.

#### Table 14: NDCs of the target countries of IGREENFIN I
| Countries | NDC Objectives |
|-----------|----------------|
| Burkina Faso (28/9/2015) | An unconditional pledge to reduce emissions by 6.6% below business-as-usual (BAU) levels by 2030, with a further 11.6% reduction conditional upon international support. Includes interim |

# B.2. Theory of change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

## 85. The climate problems that the proposed IGREENFIN I programme will address are:

i) the vulnerability of the selected agricultural value chains to climatic shocks and natural hazards (floods, droughts, diseases, intense rain, wildfires and locust outbreaks) related to the target countries’ geographic location and characteristics;  
ii) the impacts of climate change (primarily higher temperatures and reduced precipitation) and climate variability on agricultural production and productivity and water availability for agriculture, which lead to greater food insecurity, unemployment, migration and conflicts;  
iii) non-climate stressors (political instability, environmental and land degradation, urbanization, land tenure issues, etc.) that affect agricultural production and contribute to climate change;  
iv) recurrent climatic shocks, which increase the risks for financial institutions and reduce investments in key agricultural value chains; and  
v) the contributions of agriculture (livestock, forest and land use) and the energy sector to GHG emissions in the GGW region.

## 86. Moreover, there is an absence of a regional support programme and an overarching framework for GCF funded projects and partners and countries funded projects to help strengthen institutional and regulatory capacity and reporting, generate and share innovative solutions and knowledge and increase private sector financial participation in green business in the region.

## 87. Shift in the development pathway. Building the low-carbon, climate resilient market in agriculture in the GGW countries requires measures to encourage smallholder farmers to organize themselves as cooperatives, FOs and/or MSMEs so that they can collectively access concessional loans, thereby providing much needed access to financing to groups that have traditionally been financially excluded from markets. Smallholder farmers are perceived as risky and although agriculture is the backbone of the target countries’ economies and social development, there is still need for well-designed incentives that can encourage the private sector to invest more in climate resilient, low emission agriculture and bring innovation to the sector all along the GGW.

## 88. The agricultural sector offers considerable opportunities for mobilizing private investment for green businesses. In this proposal, “green business” refers to investments in the selected agricultural value chains that promote climate resilient, low emission practices in line with the GGWI objectives and the target countries’ climate change adaptation and mitigation agenda in the following seven sectors: sustainable livestock production, sustainable horticulture production (tomato), sustainable tree crop production (mango, cashew), NTFPs (shea), sustainable cereal production (rice, millet), sustainable cassava production and sustainable groundnut production.

---

### Côte d’Ivoire  
(30/09/2015)  

Côte d’Ivoire’s goal is to unconditionally lower its GHG emissions by 28% relative to a BAU emissions scenario of 34.25 MtCO2e by 2030 in energy, water, agriculture, transport, waste and industry. Adaptation target sectors are water resources, agriculture, livestock and fisheries, coastal zones, energy, land use and forestry.

### Ghana  
(11/09/2015)  

Ghana’s emission reduction goal is to unconditionally lower its GHG emissions by 15% relative to a BAU emissions scenario of 73.95 MtCO2e by 2030. Targeted sectors are energy, water, agriculture, transport, waste and industry.

### Mali  
(29/09/2015)  

Commits to reducing emissions by 29% for agriculture, 31% for energy and 21% for forests and land use, each by 2030 and in comparison to a BAU scenario. This is an average reduction of 27%. This is conditional upon international support, although around 40% of this can be met unconditionally. Includes a section on adaptation, though only for the period 2015–2020.

### Senegal  
(26/09/2015)  

An unconditional reduction in emissions of 5% by 2030, compared to BAU levels, with interim targets of 3% by 2020 and 5% by 2025. Accompanied by a conditional target subject to international financial support of 7% by 2020, 15% by 2025 and 21% by 2030, compared to BAU levels. Contains section on adaptation, as well as information on potential obstacles.

Source: [http://www4.unfccc.int/submissions/INDC/Published%20Documents](http://www4.unfccc.int/submissions/INDC/Published%20Documents)

## Figure 16. Paradigm shift for green business interventions in the GGW region, Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana

89. Key assumptions for IGREENFIN I are summarized below:  

### Assumption 1
The creation of an innovative financing facility helps overcome growing insecurity heightened by the pandemic’s restrictions and the disruptions it has caused by reducing financial market limitations (liquidity and interest rates) and promoting greater uptake of concessional loans by smallholder farmers mainly youth and women, FOs, MSMEs and cooperatives to finance their green business projects.

### Assumption 2
The adoption of climate resilient, low emission agriculture (CRLEA) by rural communities and smallholder farmers (youth and women) increases the resilience of their health, well-being, food and water security, and the implementation of agroforestry, land restoration and other CRLEA techniques will be sufficiently widespread to generate a measurable positive impact on ecosystem functions and services.

### Assumption 3
Smallholder farmers are willing to adopt alternative, sustainable water management technologies and adaptation practices for key crops and value chains, including efficient irrigation systems and RETs, which improve the availability of water for agricultural uses in rural areas and thus, their productivity levels.

### Assumption 4
Rural communities and smallholder farmers mainly youth and women are willing to adopt agroforestry techniques, land use practices and RETs and are motivated to sustain them over the programme’s duration (6 years) and lifetime (25 years). The use of these technologies and practices results in the avoidance of 5.6 million tCO2 eq., as expressed in the five countries’ NDCs.

### Assumption 5
( IGREENFIN I & GGW Regional Support Program ) IFAD and other financial and technical partners are willing to coordinate with the target countries and other stakeholders to build on GCF-funded projects to strengthen institutional capacity and reporting, generate and share innovative solutions and knowledge, and increase private sector financial participation in green business projects.

To address the above climate challenges, the five selected countries under IGREENFIN I have mapped a detailed list of green practices and technology-related green business needs. However, given that this is a nascent market, most of these technologies have not yet been made readily available and significant technology gaps remain - mainly due to the limited affordability and availability of financial instruments for this sector. Capacity, knowledge and policy gaps that hinder the full transition of agriculture to climate resilient, low carbon pathways also remain.

# B. GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.1 | PAGE 45 OF 116

## 96. Theory of Change

Against the climate impacts, the Theory of Change (ToC) below summarizes how a combination of the proposed interventions described under B3 are expected to yield maximum benefits in terms of transforming the target communities into more resilient ones through the proposed components and activities.

In the rural communities of the selected countries, to improve agricultural value chains, there is a need to address climate change impacts to stimulate a green business market. This requires establishing green business financing to facilitate better access to concessional green finance, offering technical assistance and improving coordination to help smallholder farmers, FOs, cooperatives, MSMEs and agricultural banks access knowledge and technologies on climate resilient, low emission agriculture (CRLEA) previously out of their reach and build their technical capacity to design, invest in and implement viable and scalable green business plans.

In the case of banks (including LNABs) and relevant government agencies, there is a need to establish adequate policy frameworks for green finance for climate resilient, low emission agriculture at scale in the region. There is also the need to adopt best agricultural and land use and forestry (LULUCF) practices, as well as sustainable energy to power profitable agricultural value chains.

As a result, the programme is expected to:

1. Reduce emissions by 5.6 million tCO₂e through increased low-emission energy access and power generation in the selected agricultural value chains including processing;
2. Reduce vulnerability of smallholder farmers, particularly women and youth, their ecosystems and ecosystem services to climate change;

# Table 16: Preliminary selected sustainable value chains

## Main climate risks

- **Cattle**: increasing temperatures and rainfall variability reduce food and water for animals, increase incidence of disease/pests, degrade pastureland further, affecting overall growth and reproduction.
- **Horticulture (tomato)**: heat and water stress reduce yields, affect growth and increased incidence of fruit abortion and disease
- **Mango**: water stress and extreme temperatures affect flower induction, fruit set and pollen survival, reducing yields
- **Cashew**: Rising temperatures, irregular rainfall and drought negatively affect yields and quality; warmer and more humid conditions increase incidence of pests/disease, main constraint to cashew production
- **Rice**: Erratic rainfall and high temperatures cause disruptions in growing season; greater risk of floods, water shortages, strong winds, pests and disease lead to lower yields or crop failure
- **Cassava**: Vulnerability to pests and disease due to higher temperatures
- **Groundnut**: excessive heat (above 33 ˚C) and rainfall variability reduce yields; excessive moisture increases plant mortality and disease
- **Millet**: temperature increases and rainfall variability reduce yields
- **Shea**: Heavy rainfall and wind increase risk of loss of flowers and fruit; drought, floods and wildfires increase tree mortality; drought increases risk of fruit abortion. Risk of decline in favourable habitats.

## Selected value chains Climate risks Green business evidence of impacts Adaptation and mitigation interventions Adaptation and mitigation benefits

### Sustainable livestock production

#### Cattle

Projected increases in temperatures, rainfall variability and frequency and length of droughts across the Sahel expected to reduce food and water availability for cattle, contribute to disease/pest outbreak, affect reproductive cycle (reduce calving rates from 60-70% to 25-30%) and growth, and degrade pastureland further.

10,000 cattle green businesses

Livestock disease kills 20% of ruminants in developing countries. Desertification and land degradation have resulted in loss of pastureland. Higher temperatures found to reduce feed intake, milk production, fertility and lead to energy deficits. During major droughts in the past, farmers reported that they had lost entire herds.

**Adaptation**: Investment in fodder banks; crop-livestock integration; improvements to grazing corridors; efficient watering technologies; vaccination, pest monitoring and control systems, intensification of cultivated pastures; resilient breeds.

**Mitigation**: Frequent removal of manure for composting and RETs to store vaccines.

**Adaptation**: Improved access to water, feed and disease control will contribute to greater resilience and productivity of livestock, which is key for millions of small livestock producers in target countries.

**Mitigation**: emissions from cattle production reduced through improved management of manure and use of RETs to store vaccines.

#### Chicken

Projected increases in temperatures, high humidity, sunshine intensity have negative effects on poultry production, contribute to disease/pest outbreak and affect the cost of production, the number of birds needed to raise for eggs and meat and feed grain availability. High temperatures and low rainfall affect the grain harvest, the supply of grains to the market and ultimately the cost of poultry production.

15,000 poultry green businesses

Higher temperatures have been found to reduce feed intake, egg production, lead to reduced feed grain availability. The increase in temperature ultimately increases the mortality rate of chickens grown for meat by 8.4%, laying hens by 0.84%, and indigenous chickens by 0.32%.

**Adaptation**: Climate-smart livestock genotype; crossbreeding with exotic birds; heat/drought/flood/saline resilient climate resilient farming practices; conserve & utilize climate resilient native poultry; proper feed formulation; heat resilient housing; processing of chicken waste into fertilizer; extensive poultry management; vaccination, pest monitoring and control systems.

**Mitigation**: Frequent removal of manure for composting. RETs to store vaccines.

**Adaptation**: Improved housing, health, feed and disease control will contribute to greater resilience and productivity chicken meat and egg, which is key for millions of small chicken producers in target countries.

**Mitigation**: emissions from chicken production reduced through improved management of manure and the use of RETs to store vaccines instead of fossil fuels.

# Sustainable Horticulture and Vegetable Production

## Tomato

High temperatures, excess water or drought reduce productivity levels. Heat also impacts farmers' ability to market and conserve tomatoes, which are highly perishable. Climate variability increases incidence of disease.

1,500 tomato green businesses

Studies show significant declines in yields due to heat and water stress. High temperatures affect plant growth and reproduction, with increased incidence of abortion of fruit. Lack of water results in formation of fewer flowers and affects pollen. Farmers reported poor yields due to climate change.

### Adaptation

- Drip irrigation
- Soil management techniques to address low fertility and salinity (organic fertilizers, mulch)
- Higher-yield, disease-resistant varieties

### Mitigation

- Promotion of RETs for storage and processing facilities and efficient irrigation systems to displace fossil fuel use.

## Restoring Degraded Land and Agroforestry

### Sustainable Mango with Native Species (agroforestry model 1)

Negative impacts of water stress and extreme temperatures on flower induction, fruit set and pollen survival. CC scenarios predict a rapid decline in the number of induction days by end of century. Shorter flowering periods reduce productivity. Rainfall variability – especially if there is rain during flowering period – may also reduce yields.

6,000 mango green businesses

Influence of climate change on yield, time of maturation and quality

### Adaptation

- Drip irrigation
- Organic fertilizer
- Improved pruning practices
- Integration of mango trees in agroforestry systems
- Intercropping with cereals

### Mitigation

- Mango trees have a significant capacity for carbon sequestration, tend to use up more carbon than they emit
- RETs to power mango processing equipment to reduce post-harvest losses

### Adaptation

- Mango trees serve as ecological barrier against CC
- Contribute to soil and water conservation
- Better production and harvesting techniques increase yields and farmers' incomes and contribute to job creation
- Growing mangos in agroforestry systems with native trees can increase yields threefold and prevent soil erosion

### Mitigation

- Improved soil management through agroforestry contribute to carbon sequestration
- Adoption of RETs reduces use of fossil fuels and wood as energy source and thus, emissions

## Cashew Production with Native Species (agroforestry model 2)

Increased temperature, rainfall and humidity can lead to higher incidence of pests and disease, two major constraints to cashew production. Irregular rainfall, violent winds and prolonged drought can impact yields and quality. Though highly tolerant to drought, low rainfall during vegetative stage can prevent trees from obtaining enough water to sustain flowering.

6,000 cashew green businesses

Higher temperatures, erratic rainfall and increases in humidity increase the incidence of pests and disease. Irregular rainfall and temperature changes have been found to affect production.

### Adaptation

- Mulching to retain soil moisture
- Prevent weed growth and enhance soil structure
- Organic fertilizer
- Inclusion of cashew trees in agroforestry systems
- Drip irrigation

### Investment

- In storage, conservation and processing techniques and infrastructure for reduced post-harvest losses and value addition

### Mitigation

- Cashew introduced to control soil erosion and for reforestation
- Ideal for

### Adaptation

- Improved productivity increase smallholders' incomes and create jobs
- Cashew trees contribute to soil and moisture conservation
- Prevention of erosion and slowing desertification

### Mitigation

- Improved soil management through agroforestry models using mixed native species with cashew trees contributes to carbon sequestration
- Adoption of RETs reduces use of fossil fuels and wood as energy source and thus, emissions

## Shea (community forest model)

Favourable habitats of shea trees will decline by 13% (RCP8.5) by 2070 due to climate change. Higher risk of losing flowers and fruit due to heavy rainfall and winds; drought, flooding and wildfires increasing tree mortality and inhibit harvesting; decrease in water supply affecting growth and limiting processing; more frequent and prolonged droughts leading to the abortion of fruit. Deforestation biggest threat.

5,000 shea green businessess

Higher temperatures and changes in rainfall patterns are already causing the trees to shift southward, with impacts on the trees’ growth, regeneration and production levels.

### Adaptation: climate resilient practices such as assisted natural regeneration and Zaï, sustainable forest management techniques.

### Mitigation: Agroforestry landscape in which shea is naturally grown acts like a carbon sink, controls soil erosion; adoption of RETs to power shea processing and storage facilities.

### Adaptation: Agroforestry parklands conserve natural resource base, improve soil fertility, enhance resilience and allow farmers to diversify production.

Investments in forest management and shea processing capacity improve incomes and create jobs, especially for women.

### Mitigation: shea value chain fixes 1.5 million tons of CO2 every year and every ton of shea kernels produced has a negative carbon footprint of 1.04 tons of CO2 (FAO-GSA, 2020). Valorization of shea helps reduce deforestation and thus avoid emissions.

Improved soil management through maintenance of shea trees contributes to carbon sequestration, adoption of RETs reduces use of fossil fuels and wood as energy source and thus, emissions.

## Sustainable Rice production

Increasing climate variability (rainfall) and high temperatures disrupt growing seasons. Greater risk of prolonged drought, heat waves, floods, water shortages, strong winds and storms, pests and diseases, causing substantial yield reductions or crop failure, which are higher for rainfed production. SLR increases risk of floods and salinization.

20,000 rice green businesses

Estimated reductions in rice yields range from 5-25% and up to 80%, in irrigated rice systems in the hot-dry season and around 40% in rainy season.

### Adaptation: SRI; improved short-cycle rice varieties; efficient irrigation; management of soil salinity (namely drainage, flooding and organic matter addition).

### Mitigation: adoption of RETs to power processing facilities and irrigation; SRI involves alternate wetting and drying with minimal water application, thus reducing GHG emissions from flooded rice fields.

### Adaptation: More resilient and higher yielding rice production improves farmers’ income and contributes to food security. More efficient water use.

### Mitigation: Reduced emissions from flooded rice fields thanks to SRI and adoption of RETs.

## Sustainable Millet Production

- Though well-adapted to hot areas, temperature increases higher than 2°C are projected to decrease millet yields by 10% over next 20 years and 15-25% by 2080.
- Rain variability contributes to lower yields, primarily excess rain during flowering, which can cause crop failure.
- 35,000 millet green businesses
- Production predicted to decrease on average by 10% over next 20 years.
- Adaptation: Climate resilient ag practices such as inter-cropping; soil fertility (compost) and water retention (Zai and half-moons) techniques; climate information systems.
- Mitigation: Use of millet crop leftovers to maintain and increase soil carbon levels; adoption of RETs
- Adaptation: More resilient production with higher yields increases farmers’ incomes and contributes to food security. Improved soil and water management.
- Mitigation: Crop leftovers have a high carbon content, making them particularly significant for increasing soil carbon levels.
- Remissions avoided due to adoption of RETs instead of fossil fuels.

## Theory of Change of Regional Support Programme

98. Several GCF projects and programmes are already being implemented in the GGW countries and new projects are being elaborated by Direct and International Accredited Entities. These projects contribute to achieving the GGWI’s objectives by implementing specific activities focused on one or more of the five pillars mentioned in Section B.1 (value chain development, landscape restoration, climate-smart infrastructure, capacity building and institutional development). However, in the absence on an overarching framework, these GCF projects and programmes are being implemented in isolation, resulting in missed opportunities for improved delivery of the GCF mandate. The GGW Regional Support Programme will address:  
- Lack of analysis and sharing of lessons and experiences across GCF projects in GGW countries.  
- Duplication of activities by AEs due to limited awareness of AEs and project staff of similar topics or activities being implemented in different countries by GCF and other projects including those supported by the GEF.  
- Limited evidence-based knowledge generated and tailored to specific audience to inform GCF project development.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 55 OF 116

## 138 GCF (2021), Innovation is imperative, GCF tells Leaders Climate Summit, 23 April, Press Release, https://www.greenclimate.fund/news/innovation-imperative-gcf-tells-leaders-climate-summit  

- Increased costs for AEs, countries and the GCF due to limited exchanges of knowledge on GCF project implementation and available studies (e.g., climate analysis, gender assessments, etc.)
- Limited coordination at the country level to strategically plan the allocation of GCF, GEF and other resources that can contribute to the achievement of  the GGW objectives resulting in a duplication of projects and activities. NDAs are not necessarily aware of the lessons learnt, best practices or innovation from other countries or other GCF projects, which could help them to better plan their respective Country Work Plan to the GCF, projects, iteration of their NDCs or NAPs.
- Innovative solutions, in particular digital solutions , to addressing climate change in value chains and landscape restoration developed by GCF, GEF and other stakeholders ’ projects  are not widely available and spread across the GCF project portfolio.

## 99. While innovation and digital solutions are increasingly recognized as a strategic path for addressing climate change impacts, the ecosystem to support innovation and digital solutions developers is in its very early stage of development , meaning missed opportunities for replication and scaling up.

## 100. The benefits from the implementation of the knowledge management and innovation activities will contribute to several outcomes of the GCF Updated Strategic Plan (see Figure 18 below) by creating a transparent and collaborative environment for knowledge generation at the country level and across GCF funded projects. Knowledge generated by GCF projects will be captured, analysed and shared with specific audiences to improve the efficiency and effectiveness of GCF projects , as described above. Similarly, the collaborative environment created by the GGW RSP to foster innovation will increase stakeholders’ capacities across the region to adopt and scale up innovative and digital solutions through coalitions. Increased access of GCF NDAs, GGW, GEF and AF focal points, AEs and DAEs to knowledge and innovation will increase their capacities to replicate, streamline and scale up climate activities and investments that have proven effective in similar contexts.

### Figure 18. GCF expected contribution of KM and coalitions to the Strategic Plan for Green Climate 2020 -2023

## 101. To address the challenges mentioned above, the Regional Support Programme (RSP) will improve access to best practices, encourage learning and foster innovation  and digital transformation across Great Green Wall countries so as to increase individual GCF projects ’ collective impact. The RSP’s added contribution is that its entire structure and all its actions are geared towards building the knowledge and systems necessary to identify transformational pathways for the GGW area. The RSP will not replace the knowledge management and innovation activities of each GCF project , nor take over the responsibilities of the corresponding AEs. It aims to create bridges through a structured framework to ensure that project specific information, knowledge and learning is properly captured and disseminated across GCF projects and AEs, but also GEF projects and other entities contributing to tackling climate change in the GGW. The GGW RSP will be uniquely placed as a facilitator between individual GCF projects to promote the scal ing up of existing innovation in the GGW 138 and to increase the uptake of innovations and digital solutions developed by other stakeholders in current and future GCF projects.

### B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 4 pages)

#### 102. The overall objective of this regional flagship programme (IGREENFIN I and the Regional Support Programme) is to build the resilience of smallholder farmers to current and future climate risks in the five target countries (three from the GGW [Burkina Faso, Mali and Senegal] plus Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana) by:  
- i) providing support to selected local national agricultural banks (LNABs) for the creation of green credit lines to finance the green business projects of smallholder farmers organized in FOs, cooperatives, women and youth-led organizations and MSMEs;  
- ii) addressing capacity-building, knowledge and policy gaps that hinder the transfer and adoption of adaptation and mitigation practices by green businesses, and  
- iii) enhancing the collective impact of all GCF funded projects, including IGREENFIN I, through the establishment of a coordination framework for knowledge generation and sharing, project implementation and reporting, mobilizing private investment and promoting innovation at scale to contribute to the improved resilience of ecosystems.

The flagship programme focuses on creating an enabling environment to increase the flow of funds to green businesses engaging in climate resilient, low emission agricultural practices in the GGW area and Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana. It will further contribute with a potential total GHG emission abatement of 5,637,184 million tCO2eq from the use of renewable energy technologies (RETs) and sustainable forest and land management practices. This mitigation potential represents 3.8 percent of the GGW' emission reduction target (250 MtCO2).

#### 103. The Regional Support Programme covers all GCF projects and programmes being implemented by Direct and International Access Entities only in the 11 countries of the GGW (Burkina Faso, Chad, Djibouti, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, Senegal and Sudan).

#### 104. Programme beneficiaries: The programme will directly benefit 378,600 smallholder farmers who must be organized in FOs, cooperatives, women and youth organization and MSMEs and over 2,494,000 people indirectly, of which 50 percent will be women and 50 percent youth by increasing their climate resilience through access to affordable, long-term green loans. Other beneficiaries include: 1,500 MSMEs and 2,500 FOs, cooperatives or women- or youth-led organizations, two commercial banks (Attijariwafa Bank and the Islamic Bank of Senegal) and potential LNABs partners, such as MFIs. Furthermore, 50 technologies and innovative solutions will be transferred or licensed to support climate resilient, low-emission development during the implementation of the programme.

#### 105. The direct beneficiaries of the Regional Support Programme include: GCF National Designated Authorities, GGW National Focal Points and regional entities, GEF national focal points, Accredited Entities, country project teams and key

## 106. The programme has three components: (I) Green Business Financing Facility (Loan); (II) Technical Assistance Facility (Grant) and (III) the Regional Support Programme (Grant). This programme is in line with each of the five selected countries’ Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC), as well as local climate policy initiatives as outlined under Section D.5.

## 107. Component 1 – Green Business Financing Facility (Pillars 1, 2, 3 and 4 of the GGW Accelerator) will be established in each of the five selected LNABs to provide highly concessional loans to FOs, women and youth-led organizations, cooperatives and MSMEs (including agribusiness dealers and photovoltaic operators) to finance the adoption of best adaptation and mitigation practices in the selected agricultural value chains in each target country and to address key climate hazards such floods, water scarcity, wildfire and drought, among others. These include, for instance, efficient irrigation techniques for both rainy and dry seasons, sustainable rice intensification, ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) or nature-based solutions and mitigation measures such as solar energy to power the selected value chains, among others (refer to Table 8). Of the EUR73,971 million from the GCF loan, EUR49,314 million will be provided as a loan at highly concessional rates to promote adaptation practices, and the remaining EUR24,657 million of GCF resources will be allocated to mitigation practices (renewable energy use in the 10 selected agriculture value chains).

## 108. The beneficiaries of the loans (Component 1) are FOs, cooperatives and MSMEs. The sub-borrowers include also Attijariwafa Bank which operates only in Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Mali and Senegal; the Islamic Bank of Senegal identified during the design and other Intermediary Financial Institutions (IFIs) to be identified at implementation stage. Eligibility criteria for Attijariwafa Bank, the Islamic Bank of Senegal and other IFIs to access loan proceeds are described in Annex 21 Operations Manual and in the project description. ## 109. Definition of smallholder farmers: The definition of smallholder agriculture varies from one country to another. For this programme, the mostly widely used definition of smallholder farmers was adopted, which is farmers who farm less than 2.0 hectares.

## 110. Definition of Farmer Organizations (FOs)

These organizations shall have: (i) a defined membership, (ii) a purpose for assembling, and (iii) an organizational structure in place to support members pursuing individual and collective interests.

They may differ in their: (i) scale of membership (e.g., from national federations to local associations), (ii) function (e.g., policy, economic, technical and/or local development or advocacy), and (iii) legal designation (e.g., registered as cooperatives, clubs, group or associations), among others. In the host country and the targeted regions in general, they combine their forces to improve bargaining power, particularly on pricing, achieve economies of scale in operations and/or improve quality control and access to financing and markets.

## 111. Definition of cooperative: An autonomous association of people who come together voluntarily to meet common economic, social and cultural aspirations and needs within a collective enterprise where power is exercised democratically. For this project, it is an enterprise which seeks a fair balance between profit and meeting the needs and interests of its members and their communities. Agricultural cooperatives allow their members to seize new economic opportunities by offering them a range of services in the agricultural sector.

### Category Quantitative Aspects

#### Medium enterprise
Employs between 10 and 100 people; subscription to the chamber of commerce; internal accounting system in place; annual profit above 50 million XOF, but not exceeding 500 million XOF140; minimum investment of 10 million XOF

#### Small enterprise
Employs between four and 10 people; subscription to the chamber of commerce; internal accounting system in place; annual profit above 10 million XOF, but not exceeding 50 million XOF; minimum investment of 1 million XOF

#### Very small and micro enterprises
Employs between one and three people; subscription to the chamber of commerce; internal accounting system in place; annual profit not exceeding 10 million XOF

### Table 19: Quantitative variables for defining MSMEs in Ghana

#### Category Quantitative aspects

##### Medium enterprise
Employs between 30 and 100 people; has a total annual turnover in the range of GHS138,000 to GHS 352,000; and with fixed assets not exceeding $1,000,000.

##### Small enterprise
Employs between 10 to 29 people; has a total annual turnover in the range of GHS 15,000 to below GHS 138,000; and with fixed assets not exceedingly more than $100,000.

##### Very small and micro enterprises
Employs between one to nine people; has a total annual turnover between GHS2,400 and GHS15,000; and with fixed assets not exceeding $10,000.

Source: Adopted and modified from Afful-Koomson et al. (2014) and AWAFA (2019).

### 113. This programme uses a combination of the definitions above to determine the eligibility of MSMEs for support: MSMEs eligible for this programme will be all those that employ from one to 100 employees and have financial needs of up to 500 million XOF. In addition, given that the programme targets youth- and woman-led MSMEs, the following additional criteria will apply and help identify them as priorities for support in all five countries. These criteria will be further refined during the first year of implementation, as specified in the OM:

#### i) For women-led MSMEs:
- Must be an officially registered agribusiness enterprise, with more than 51 per cent of its assets, holding or shares owned by woman. This will be assessed and validated with firm registration information from the relevant public registry and their respective share of profits

- Minimum firm size of three employees, of which 60 percent are women
- Activities are mainly carried out along the agricultural value chain
- Track record of loan repayments
- Bank account must be operational for more than two years
- Records and bookkeeping systems in place
- Agriculture land holding of between two and 10 hectares, and
- Any other characteristics deemed necessary by the LNABs, subject to the Executing Entity's confirmation.

#### ii) For youth-led MSMEs:
- A formally registered agribusiness enterprise with more than 51 per cent of its assets, holding or shares owned by youth, defined as individuals between 18 and 35 years of age. This will be assessed and validated based on firm registration information from the relevant public registry and their respective share of profits
- At least 30 per cent of board of director members or individuals in senior management positions are youth. This will be assessed and validated based on firm registration information from the relevant public registry
- Minimum firm size of three employees, of which 60 per cent are youth
- Activities are mainly carried out along the agricultural value chain

## Definitions:

- All destinations of the planned investments for which a loan is requested must be justified within the framework of **IGREENFIN I** and have the final goal of increasing resilience and reducing risk exposure of the MSME, cooperatives and FOs to the negative impacts of climate change and climate variability.

- **Working capital**: working capital will be understood as the available cash flow necessary for the administrative, financial and operative management of the MSMEs, cooperatives and FOs.

- **Fixed asset**: any tangible investment that the MSME will use for a considerable period that is not acquired for the purpose of selling them and that is acquired, constructed or used to adopt adaptation measures to increase the climate resilience of the MSME, cooperatives and FOs and enhance ecosystem services.

- **Productive unit**: the place where the business of the final beneficiary is located.

- **Commercial unit**: the place where the final beneficiary stores or sells his or her products.

## Eligible local national agricultural banks (LNABs)

122. Eligible banks for the implementation of the GCF loan proceeds (Component 1) that have been subject to capacity assessments and criteria for selection are:  
- The Agricultural Bank of Burkina Faso,  
- The National Investment Bank of Côte d’Ivoire,  
- The ARB Apex Bank Limited of Ghana,  
- The Agricultural Bank of Mali,  
- The Agricultural Bank of Senegal.  

They will be the primary recipients of the GCF loan proceeds.

## Eligible Intermediary Financial Institutions (IFIs)

123. IFIs will be selected following the Accredited Entity’s procedures, and criteria and loans will be provided to such IFIs in accordance with all relevant requirements stipulated in the AMA and the FAA. The same conditions used for the LNABs will be applied to IFIs.

## Commercial banks and microfinance institutions

124. Definition of commercial bank: A commercial bank is a financial institution whose basic activities are oriented mainly towards individuals (deposits, investments, savings and credit), businesses or public authorities. They can be physical establishments (branches, distributors) or virtual establishments (online or remote presence only). Their capital is held by shareholders who are not necessarily clients. This programme targets commercial banks working in the agricultural sector and smallholder farming in the host countries.

125. Definition of microfinance institutions (MFI): A microfinance institution is a local structure (NGO, association, cooperative, etc.) that delivers financial services to populations without access to the traditional banking system. MFIs mainly offer microcredit, but some also offer other types of services such as savings, micro-insurance, support and training services to deliver both social and economic benefits, since MFIs must be sustainable and financially balanced.

126. The initial commercial banks eligible to implement the loan proceeds (Component 1) are: the Islamic Bank for Senegal and the Attijariwafa Bank.

## Other Intermediary Financial Institutions (IFIs)

127. IFIs may be private or public banks or MFIs that will act as intermediaries between the eligible LNABs under section II and the final beneficiaries and will ensure that financial resources are channelled between them in accordance with the same GCF conditions and the financial terms and conditions applied by the LNAB in each country. Eligible LNABs will enter into partnership with IFIs only in areas where a LNAB does not have any representation in targeted rural areas.

128. The eligibility criteria for selection of IFIs are as follows:  
- Have extensive experience in deploying financing towards economic sectors that could potentially include adaptation activities  
- Have a demonstrable track record of working with MSMEs, cooperatives, FOs and smallholders in the targeted countries  
- Have satisfactory experience working as retailer with the eligible LNABs  
- Must have a portfolio of programmes dedicated to agriculture (agriculture, livestock, agroforestry), food processing and energy use  
- Must undertake financial services in the programme’s target areas where selected LNABs are not represented  
- Must have a monitoring system in place to track the flow of funds between LNABs and the beneficiaries and operational track record of loan repayments  
- Must be an IFI authorized by the Central Bank of West African States (BCEAO) to operate in Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Mali and Senegal and by the Central Bank of Ghana  
- Must apply the same GCF conditions and lending terms that the LNABs apply to the end users  
- Any other characteristics deemed necessary by LNABs and aligned with the GCF.

129. The analysis for an IFI’s eligibility to the green lines of credit will be carried out during the preparation and analysis stage of each LNAB's project cycle. In this stage, the IFI’s credit worthiness will be analysed according to the type of institution it is.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.1 | PAGE 64 OF 116

Using a credit risk methodology to assign it a credit quota which it may later use via the different intermediated credit programmes.

130. Each one of the above credit risk methodologies analyses areas such as the institution's capital, the quality of its assets, efficiency, rate of return and liquidity. For each area, financial indicators are used to determine the IFI’s credit risk rating. Among the most important indicators there is financial vulnerability and delinquency, which reflect the quality of the institution’s assets; the health and performance of the IFI is measured based on such indicators.

131. Also, relevant documents on environmental and social aspects of the IFI’s operations will be reviewed to study and mitigate environmental and social risks. Such analysis takes into account two types of impacts: direct and indirect. Direct impacts derive from the institution's own activities while indirect ones stem from the projects included in the IFI’s loan portfolio; the latter defines the level of the institution's environmental and social risk.

132. An environmental and social risk analysis will be carried out for each financial institution. It will review the size and probability of direct and indirect impacts of the IFI, assign it to a category of environmental and social risk and identify existing controls to mitigate the risks, as well as any additional measures that will need to be established to minimize, mitigate or compensate for risks. This process will inform the development of an Environmental Impact Study and Plan (EISP), which stipulates the measures that the IFI shall take to manage the social and environmental risks and/or impacts stemming from its operations and those of its clients. To determine if the IFI is eligible for financing through the programme’s green credit lines, the EISP and its credit rating is submitted to the LNAB’s credit committee, which is comprised by different technical areas of the selected LNAB. After approval by the credit committee, this analysis is submitted to the LNAB for final approval.

133. The LNAB is responsible for managing the direct relationship with the client and the underlying financing operations – from the identification and preparation stage until the loan is repaid in full – in order to stay adequately and timely informed of any relevant, quantitative and/or qualitative event that could affect the client's business or operations, credit rating and compliance with their contractual obligations to the LNAB. The PIU of the LNABs could recommend to the NEEs to turn down the applications of clients deemed financially non-viable after assessment. The National Steering Committee chair by the NEEs will provide an opinion on the financially non-viable clients but the final decision will be taken by the NEEs.

134. The LNAB’s follow-up credit area will make periodic visits to the IFI at least once a year. The visits must be duly documented in the client's credit file. The IFI’s obligations to the LNAB, derived from the credit agreement, will have equal rank with other liabilities of borrowers of the same gender, nature or type. In no case shall such credits, regardless of their nature, have a payment priority superior to that maintained with the LNAB. IFIs will be trained on climate change adaptation aspects as well as monitoring and evaluation of adaptation measures and the implementation arrangements at the beginning of the programme and will receive training regularly throughout the IGREENFIN I programme.

135. As the Accredited Entity, IFAD will provide support to the PMU s and PIUs during the first years to ensure that the IFIs in the targeted countries receive adequate training. ## 138

It is expected that a growing pipeline of green business projects will enable the LNABs to start aligning their portfolios with their respective country’s NDCs while building their internal capacity to leverage more concessional investments to support the uptake of climate resiliencies, low emission agriculture in the region.

## 139

Key outputs of Component 1 are:

- Output 1.1: Viable green businesses run by FOs, youth and women led-MSMEs and cooperatives created, which attract funding, and commercialized RET technologies are adopted
- Output 1.2: Technical support and capacities built for FOs, MSMEs, cooperatives, LNABs and IFIs

## 140

Key activities under component I are:

- Activity 1.1.1: Establish a Green Business Financing Facility (revolving lending facility) - window 1 (adaptation, pillars 1, 2 and 4) to catalyse adequate finance for viable green businesses in key value chains (livestock – cattle, sheep, goats and poultry; horticulture – tomatoes; mango; cashews; shea; rice; millet; groundnuts and cassava) for investment in the adoption of CRLEA practices, including land restoration techniques.

- Activity 1.1.2: Establish Green Business Financing Facility (revolving lending facility) - window 2 (mitigation, pillars 3) to catalyse adequate finance for renewable energy equipment and technologies to power the selected value chains. IGREENFIN I will promote the provision of solar energy for the following activities: (i) drip irrigation systems to address declines in agricultural yields, (ii) processing techniques to reduce post-harvest losses, and (iii) storage of vaccines to address growing livestock diseases due to climate change. Solar mini grids that power efficient drip irrigation systems are needed to address water scarcity and rainfall variability, particularly during periods of drought. Additionally, solar mini grids that power the processing, storage and packaging of agricultural products will enable beneficiaries to seize new and existing marketing opportunities, and gradually lead to reductions in the use of fossil fuel and wood in the target countries. The demand for energy for agricultural processing and transformation within IGREENFIN I’s scope of work in Burkina Faso, Mali and Senegal is estimated at 4,606 MWh per year, and 3,456 MWh per year in Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana.

- Activity 1.1.3: Provide technical assistance to set up the Green Business Financing Facility with green credit line windows 1 and 2. This activity will support LNABs in setting up the facility and its two windows within each bank and developing a platform which will include all the eligibility criteria for assessing green business proposals, climate projections and priorities per country. Under this activity, the staff of the PIU will be regularly trained to better assess all green loan applications submitted to the LNABs.

## 142. Component 2: Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) – Pillars 2, 3 & 5 (EUR 23,013 2million of the GCF Grant) will be created to address capacity, knowledge and policy gaps hindering the uptake of green business projects in the selected countries. It will target LNABs, central banks and clients (FOs, MSMEs and cooperatives), while placing a special focus on women and youth. The TAF will provide technical support to Direct Access Entities such as i) the Agricultural Bank of Senegal to build its internal capacity to mobilize additional concessional climate financing during the implementation of IGREENFIN I, and ii) the Centre de Suivi Ecologique (CSE), a strategic IGREENFIN I partner, to enhance its capacity to better monitor land use and forest management activities in all selected countries. The TAF will also support strategic partnerships between the LNABs and Attijariwafa Bank (a GCF accredited commercial bank) to crowd in additional private sector financing into the programme in the following countries: Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Mali and Senegal. The TAF will provide the necessary technical support to the Central Bank of West Africa n States (BCEAO) for Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Mali and Senegal, and the Central Bank of Ghana for the development of strategic studies and knowledge to address policy and regulatory gaps and create an enabling environment for the uptake of green business projects. This component will contribute to Pillar 2 (sustainable management of ecosystems and land restoration), Pillar 3 (climate resilient infrastructures and access to renewable energy) and Pillar 5 (capacity-building) of the Great Green Wall Accelerator. The TAF will be implemented by the respective ministries of agriculture in each country through an agreement with the NEEs, as described in Section A.20 above.

## 143. The expected outcome is enhanced capacity of LNABs and other financial institutions, national institutions and recipients to finance and implement climate resilient, low emission agriculture. As the programme adopts a regional approach, capacity-building exchanges between countries will be undertaken between the financial institutions and between MSMEs, FOs, and cooperatives from the different participating countries (events, visits, exchanges, etc.).

## 145. Eligible Technical Assistance Services Providers (TASPs) contracted by each IFAD baseline investment:

In each country, IGREENFIN I will identify organizations in the country and the region that are qualified to provide technical assistance to the LNABs, MFIs, commercial banks and MSMEs, FOs and cooperatives, including solar operators. Their training activities will be focused on the capacity-building needs identified during consultations and will provide a detailed introduction of all the important aspects of the programme. It should be noted that these organizations have not yet been selected. Consultants from the identified network of TASPs will be hired depending on the specific needs of the activity and/or beneficiaries.

## 146. TASPs should be qualified to give the following training content:

- Climate issues (risks, forecast technologies)
- Climate resilient, low emission agriculture (adaptation and mitigation measures, green technologies, soil and water management, etc.)
- Financial literacy, business plans and management
- Transition towards green finance
- Institutional and regulatory framework
- Promotion of knowledge management, South-South cooperation, exchanges

## 147. Applicable rules for technical assistance and capacity-building activities:

- All technical assistance processes shall start with the collection of data on the needs of eligible recipients from the recipients themselves.
- When submitting documentation for approval, the original copy of all documents must be presented. The time and date of the submission must be recorded on all documentation.
- No approval process will be started unless all requirements are fulfilled.
- A checklist will be provided to ensure compliance with all requirements specified in the programme’s OM (Annex 21).
- Only forms filled out using a computer or typing machine will be accepted.
- All technical assistance must be assessed by the Technical Assistance Committee appointed by the NEEs in coordination with the National Steering Committee. Any decisions or action adopted by the TAC will be subject to a favourable vote or non-objection by the NEEs. An official record must be written and shall include a description of all evaluation variables listed in the OM. The signatures of all reviewers or committee members must be included in this official record.
- The Technical Assistance Committee shall be representative of all stakeholders involved in the process.
- The majority of consultants contracted to provide technical assistance must be nationals from the country in question, with a quota of at least 50% of women.

## Component 2 has two main outputs:

### Output 2.1 – Generate knowledge, conduct studies, support knowledge transfer, provide training and monitor climate impacts

#### 148. The green business market has yet to be created in the GGW region and many technologies and practices required for climate resilient, low emission agriculture are still at a nascent stage, meaning that significant capacity and knowledge gaps remain. In addition, to make green business projects commercially viable, a better understanding is required of both market opportunities and the technology needs of FOs, women and youth-led organizations, cooperatives and MSMEs. The activities under this output will contribute to incremental capacity and knowledge building so as to accelerate the adoption of climate adaptation and mitigation practices and technologies. By increasing the capacities of FOs, women and youth-led organizations, cooperatives and MSMEs, this output strives to contribute to the restoration of 200,000 ha of degraded areas and increase smallholder farmers’ yields for the following crops: millet, cassava, rice and groundnut. It will promote the adoption of best climate resilient agricultural and land use practices, such as intercropping cereals with legumes and fruit and other trees in agroforestry systems, best practices in fruit and NTFP chains (shea butter, cashews and mango, etc.), efficient irrigation and soil conservation techniques, etc. Technical assistance (TA) will also be provided to FOs, women and youth-led organizations, cooperatives and MSMEs to prepare viable green business plans and tried and tested practices will be compiled in a catalogue of the best adaptation and mitigation solutions available in each country and across the region.

#### 149. At the level of the LNABs, training will be offered to the banks’ officials, directors and staff to strengthen their capacity to identify potential clients, assess potential beneficiaries' green businesses against the eligibility criteria and identify viable bankable green business plans and pipelines. The IFAD baseline PMUs (under the ministries of agriculture) will use the grant portion in coordination with the LNABs' PIU to hire the relevant consultants and TASPs to support LNABs and selected

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSALV.2.1 | PAGE 68 OF 116

## Output 2.1: Enhance green lending and climate risk management

The program will support partners in the area of green lending and climate risk management. FOs, cooperatives and MSMEs will be recipients of the TA proceeds. Training will be provided on how to conduct market assessments, data collection and analysis, the selection of target clients, preliminary eligibility and creditworthiness assessment s of potentially viable business plans proposed by FOs, cooperatives and MSMEs, including ones focused on the acquisition and use of renewable energy equipment required along the agricultural value chains. Additionally, the programme will support the installation of solar mini grids for food processing, storage and packaging to enable smallholders to seize marketing opportunities, which will gradually lead to reduced use of fossil fuel and wood for energy in the target countries.

The TAF will assist with the creation of a package of tools and instruments to better identify inv estment risk s and increase awareness and training in climate resilient, low emission agriculture (CRLEA) practices. The programme will support LNABs and partners (MFIs and commercial banks) in developing and updating their environmental and risk management systems (ESMS) and skills, environmental and climate strategies and related plans. Activities for this output include (for activities disaggregated by country, see the appendix “Activities breakdown per country”):

- **Activity 2.1.1.** Provide capacity-building to FOs, women and youth- led organizations, cooperatives and MSMEs on understanding climate risks and climate resilient, low emission practices and integrating them into green business plans
- **Activity 2.1.2.** Offer capacity-building on the development of bankable green business plans  
- **Activity 2.1.3.** Provide training to FOs, MSMEs and cooperatives on how to implement financed green business plans for results
- **Activity 2.1.4.** Support women and youth-led FOs and MSMEs to increase their participation, improve their financial literacy, natural resources management capacity, involvement in decision-making processes, ability to use labo ur-saving and cost-competitive CRLEA technologies and support for gender-related studies to guide decision-making.
- **Activity 2.1.5.** Provide technical assistance on green lending and climate risk management to LNABs  
- **Activity 2.1.6.** Provide technical assistance to enhance governance and management of loan products, fill the green credit lines policy gap and ensure use in each LNAB and IFI.
- **Activity 2.1.7.** Develop a package of tools and instruments , including ESMS, for LNABs to assess green business plans  
- **Activity 2.1.8.** Conduct awareness-raising and training on climate resilient, low emission agriculture within LNABs.

## Output 2.2: Improve policy dialogue, government technical and institutional capacity, advocacy, training, knowledge management, information dissemination and stakeholder management

This output will promote a transformative approach and a feedback loop for scaling up investments in green business through enhanced public and private sector engagement and partnerships and the provision of capacity-building and training for national institutions on the economic development objectives associated with the two financing windows. Furthermore, it will strengthen knowledge management, information dissemination and stakeholder engagement through the creation of solution-oriented platforms, including a databank on innovative projects, that will be used to inform and facilitate a high-level policy dialogue between FOs, LNABs , MFIs and government authorities (national and local). With regards to renewable energy in agriculture, institutional and regulatory frameworks and planning on renewable energy will be improved to better support knowledge sharing, capitalization and dissemination of lessons learned.

The programme’s impact, institutionalization and replicability will be enhanced through the involvement of the relevant sector ministries (energy, environment and agriculture, economy and finance) responsible for national and sector policies, planning and strategies. The programme will also organize high-level and technical events and develop a platform for exchange between value chain actors and financial institutions. To support stakeholder engagement and capacity-building on green lending, a stakeholder engagement plan will be developed.

To promote the uptake of technologies and adaptation at scale, the programme will promote South-South and Triangular cooperation with similar programmes in Latin America and Asia financed by the GCF (e.g., AfD-GCF project on financing systems , the Productive Investment Initiative for Adaptation to Climate Change, or CAMBio II , in Central America, the Niger Inclusive Green Financing Project). Other activities include: the production of 40 policy briefs and five publications and catalogues for decision makers. With the support of the Regional Support Program me (Component 3), IGREENFIN I will promote the continued use of green finance worldwide and connect the selected LNABs with similar initiatives around the world.

To ensure sustainability beyond the programme’s life cycle, technical assistance will be provided to

## 152. To incentivize the sector, the TAF will launch a Sahel & GGW Award. The Sahel & GGW Award process consists of: i) the granting of an Annual Sahel Award incentive in the form of debt cancellation to promote sustainability in smallholder agriculture, and ii) the holding of an institutionalized annual award ceremony. The Award will be handed out to the five best projects in each country submitted during the year under each funding window described in Component 1 (10 projects per year per country). For each winner, a maximum of EUR 6986, 15 of debt on top of the loan principal will be cancelled. The prize will be administered as follows: EUR 3287, 6 will be cancelled at the time of selection; EUR 2465, 7 at the time of the midterm review, and EUR 1232, 85 once adaptation/mitigation measures have been successfully implemented and monitored. As the administrator of the award, the recipient government (NEE) will hold the right to revoke the debt forgiveness to recipients who at the time of the midterm review have not complied with original terms. The Sahel & GGW Award trophies will be given to the selected beneficiaries and the award will be considered collateral when applying for additional loans from banks. The recipient country will administer these resources through its Ministry of Finance. IFAD and selected banks will review applications and make recommendations to the government based on their expert opinion. The award selection committee will be further detailed in the implementation manual, but decisions will remain with the governments. Measures will also be taken to ensure that among the winners of the award, 50 per cent are MFIs, FOs, cooperatives and MSMEs led by women and 50 per cent are led by youth. The Annual Sahel & GGW Award Ceremony will be organized to honour and give greater visibility to changes in attitudes and behavioural changes and the best adaptation and mitigation projects and initiatives developed at the national level by FOs, cooperatives, women and youth organizations and MSMEs. A Sahel & GGW Award Trophy will also be designed. Also, under this output, women and youth will be incentivized to implement climate adaptation and mitigation measures and RET in agricultural value chains. Targeting will be carried out according to the definition of women- and youth-led MSMEs, FOs and cooperatives in each country (see above).

- Activity 2.2.2. Organize high-level round table events and exchanges on green finance
- Activity 2.2.3. Organize South-South and triangular cooperation visits and tours for experience sharing
- Activity 2.2.4. Enhance knowledge management and information sharing
- Activity 2.2.5. Organize annual Sahel & GGW Award event
- Activity 2.2.6. Organize events to replenish the Sahel & GGW Award
- Activity 2.2.7. Develop application protocols and framework
- Activity 2.2.8. Enrol women and youth entrepreneurs to compete for the Sahel & GGW Award.

## 141 IAP Midterm review report draft

142 GEF Secretariat (2021), GEF-6 Commodities IAP Program: Emerging Lessons from the Good Growth Partnership,  
http://assembly.thegef.org/publications/gef-6-commodities-iap-program-emerging-lessons-good-growth-partnership  

regional programmes that aim to strengthen the impacts of individual projects show that these projects give relatively little priority to knowledge management, particularly in their initial phases. This often results in delays in implementation due to loss of information or the repetition of common errors from previous projects implemented by other partners or in other countries. Projects usually do not have dedicated knowledge management experts on staff and M&E officers are often not well equipped to conduct knowledge management processes. KM is almost never part of the TORs of country staff, nor are there clear KM indicators and targets for the activities 141.

An overarching global knowledge management approach will not only strengthen exchanges among projects and countries but also make project and programme implementation more efficient and effective by ensuring that existing knowledge is appropriated from the early stages on.

## 156. The GCF GGW Regional Support Programme managed by IFAD will contribute to the effective management of the knowledge generated by GCF and GEF projects by providing faster and more effective access to relevant internal and external knowledge to maximize impact in the GGW.

The GGW RSP Knowledge Management component will foster an enhanced culture of knowledge sharing whose benefits include:

- Easy access to information and resources for GCF NDAs ; GGW, GEF and AF focal points ; AEs and especially DAEs. With information more readily available to them, these actors and entities will be better equipped to support countries in implementing their NDCs and NAPS through present and future GCF projects.
- Increased pace of learning uptake. Well-targeted learning events such as South-South exchanges have proven to be an effective means of facilitating peer-to-peer learning among experts from different countries 142 . Sharing experiences from countries where project implementation is more advanced will help teams accelerate the progress of less mature projects, which will be able to take advantage of existing best practices and effective approaches.
- Knowledge for reuse: Reusing ideas, documents and expertise minimizes the need to reinvent the wheel, avoids repeating problems and mistakes, saves time and accelerates progress, as it enables teams to adapt successful experiences and good practices to the context they are working in. For instance, some of the background assessments elaborated by AEs and reviewed by the GCF Secretariat and iTAP for a specific FP could be reused or adapted by another AE.
- Opportunities to take advantage of existing expertise and experience: The more complementary the expertise of the team members, the greater the power of the team.
- Identification and dissemination of methods, tools, templates, techniques, and examples that can serve as the building blocks for supporting processes and procedures. Using these consistently streamlines work, improves quality and enhances overall efficiency.
- Data for evidence-based decision-making by NDAs, DAEs and international AEs, and GGW, GEF and AF focal points: Using knowledge generated from the analysis of data, especially when it is directed to specific audiences, allows teams to make decisions based on actual experiences and lessons learned.
- Effective KM will promote operational excellence, technical innovation and efficiency gains for NDAs, GCF projects under implementation and AEs. Innovations are shared systematically and timely among projects, countries and entities, allowing for faster replication and scaling up.

## Knowledge management structure

157. GCF projects have KM strategies and activities in place and have allocated financial and human resources to achieve the proposed results. The support for knowledge management provided by the Regional Support Programme will not replace the existing KM strategies of each GCF project or the responsibilities of the corresponding AEs. On the contrary, it aims to build bridges between all these project-specific KM strategies and resources and create a structured framework to ensure that information and knowledge is properly captured and shared systematically with other GCF projects, entities and GGW countries.

The Regional Support Programme will provide support at two levels:

- **Regional level:** structure, streamline, analyse and disseminate knowledge generated by GCF projects across the GGW countries through a KM strategy, a knowledge centre, specific knowledge and communication products and the organization of events. In addition, synergies with the GGW Accelerator, GEF and AF will be created at the regional level for specific activities described below. The Regional Support Programme Unit will hire a Regional Knowledge Management Specialist who will be in charge of coordinating activities at the regional level.
- **Country level:** facilitate and support GCF projects in sharing the knowledge generated by each project and fostering linkages and learning based on past and present projects in the country. The RSP will arrange for a Knowledge Management Specialist to be hosted by the NDA in each country,

## 158 Key activities for Output 3.1 are:

- **Activity 3.1.1** Establish a knowledge baseline. The rationale goal here is to map all relevant information on climate financing within GGW countries and determine where relevant knowledge is missing.
- **Activity 3.1.2** Develop a Knowledge and Communication Strategy. Based on the knowledge baseline assessment, the RSP Support Unit will work with all AEs implementing projects in the GGW to build a knowledge management and communication strategy
  - The strategy will cover the role of KM and communication for the GGW RS P (external communication, internal communication, gathering, analysing, documenting, translating information, adapting information), strategic objectives, target audiences, coordination of products to be defined, timeline for reporting knowledge, templates, KM specific indicators, and responsibilities for individual projects.
  - This activity will respond to the challenges faced by most regional programmes (IAP, BRICKS), namely the limited resources allocated to KM in each project, the implementation of projects in an isolated manner and variations in institutional standards.

- **Activity 3.1.3** Create a knowledge centre. A knowledge centre will be created to execute the knowledge management strategy. It will make knowledge and information on key thematic areas and country project activities readily available to multiple stakeholders and easy to disseminate. It will organize knowledge into the GGW RSP’s five pillars, and by thematic areas (M&E, communication), project or country, etc.
  - New and existing knowledge disseminated through this centre will include good practices, improved indigenous knowledge, CRLEA, land restoration, value chain development, climate-resilient infrastructures, water management, etc.
  - It will collect existing gender assessments, climate assessments and legal analyses prepared by AEs for GCF Funding Proposals and GEF project proposals and make them available to all AEs, especially DAEs, in the GGW countries.
  - The knowledge centre will function mainly in English

Create communities of practice. Communities of practices (CoP) will help connect practitioners from the projects/programmes of GCF, GEF, AF and other AEs aligned with the GGW Accelerator’s five pillars so that they may learn from experiences and projects and incentivize cross-regional knowledge exchanges. Based on IFAD’s experience in this area146, CoPs are effective in identifying best solutions and lessons learned based on evidence and in disseminating information on them, which help to scale up and ensure faster and more efficient delivery of GCF projects. The RSP’s regional and national KM experts will support the creation and facilitation of CoPs to generate a constructive environment of mutual support and trust among the experts and other stakeholders. The CoPs will be set up: i) at the country level to connect the KM experts of GCF and GEF projects147 and programmes implemented in the country by different AEs who will structure and systematize the exchange of information, good practices, lessons learned and innovation among those projects, and draft policy recommendations with the GCF NDA, the GEF and AF national focal points, and the GGW focal point, and ii) at the regional level, mainly to enhance exchanges between countries in the region and organize South-South exchanges.

## Activity 3.1.5
Generate knowledge products to disseminate lessons learned and scalable good practices and inform evidence-based investment and policymaking. The RSP will bring added value to the existing knowledge from individual GCF and GEF projects148 by creating knowledge products that facilitate the analysis of experiences in different countries and projects and the dissemination of the knowledge across the 11 countries. The result of this analysis will be a set of good practices that can be easily adapted to and adopted in similar contexts and scaled up across the region. In addition, data gathered across the 11 countries will be used to develop specific knowledge products that target investors and policymakers to inform evidence-based decision-making. The specific knowledge products such as policy briefs, social media pages, monthly newsletters, internal bulletins and tool kits to be developed under this component will be defined in the Knowledge Management and Communication Strategy (see activity 3.1.2). Through regular interaction with the RSP KM specialists in each of the 11 GGW countries and with all GCF and GEF projects in the GGW countries, as well as desk reviews, portfolio reviews and existing academic and public/private sector research, this activity will methodically gather, capture and share lessons learned and best practices from all the key result areas of the GCF and relevant GEF projects which are contributing to the objectives of the GGW. The Country Knowledge Management Specialist will be in charge of drafting country level knowledge products and the Regional Knowledge Management Specialist will be coordinating the creation of regional products149.
