### Annual Regional Knowledge Share Fairs
To bring together the knowledge and lessons learned from other GGW partners such as the Permanent Interstate Committee for Drought Control in the Sahel (CILSS) and independent units such as Agrhymet, the Sahara and Sahel Observatory (OSS), IUCN, the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS), FAO, the EU and other international partners, UNCCD, UNEP, World Agroforestry Centre (ICRAF), as well as knowledge providers such as the World Overview of Conservation Approaches and Technologies (WOCAT) and the Africa Regreening Initiative, among others. Knowledge Share Fairs are facilitated events designed to share knowledge in an interactive way. The key to a Share Fair’s success lies in the linkages and connections people make at the event. Share Fairs bridge social, hierarchical and technological gaps through various participatory formats. The participatory aspect of the events also contributes to the generation of a community around the themes discussed.

### South-South Exchanges
Will be organized to share the best scalable good practices from other regions. These events will include training and periodic study tours of GCF, GEF and AF projects.

## Activity 3.1.7. Enhance coordination and programming among GCF NDAs, AEs and GEF, AF and GGW national focal points

At the regional level, the Regional KM Specialist will seek to build synergies between the Regional Support Programme and the GEF/UNEP regional project “Harnessing the Great Green Wall Initiative for a Sustainable and Resilient Sahel” (and the future GEF8 programme when defined) through ongoing dialogue to ensure the complementarity of activities and the mobilization of financing. At the country level, coordination committees will be established by building on relevant existing committees and bringing together NDAs, GGW, GEF and AF focal points, relevant sectoral ministries and AEs. The coordination committees will aim to streamline the development of GCF Country Work Plans, GEF STAR Allocations and the countries’ NDCs and contribute to the design of impactful climate-related projects by sharing work programmes, information, knowledge and updates on portfolios at both the design and implementation phases. The Country Knowledge Management Specialists will coordinate the meetings and facilitate the activities of the Coordination Committees.

## Output 3.2 Innovation and digital transformation technologies mapped and ecosystem built

### 159 In this proposal, innovation is defined as finding and applying new approaches to address existing problems or serve unmet needs. From a development perspective, an innovation is a new solution with the transformative ability to accelerate impact. Innovation can be fuelled by science and technology, entail improved ways of working with new and diverse partners or involve new social and business models or policy, creative financing mechanisms or path-breaking improvements in delivering essential services and products. Innovation has been and will be pivotal for reaching sustained, scalable solutions to the world’s most complex problems, including climate change.

### 160 Adopted in 2015, the Paris Agreement stipulates that accelerating, encouraging and enabling innovation is critical for an effective, long-term global response to climate change and promoting economic growth and sustainable development. The growing innovations associated with digital technologies create new opportunities for information-sharing and alternative forms of connectivity, financing models and governance arrangements.

### 161 According to UN and other experts, artificial intelligence and digital technology could bring a 10-20 per cent reduction in global carbon dioxide emissions by 2030. Disruptive climate solutions for technology, policy, finance, business models, leadership and capacity-building are key to accelerating climate resilient,

## • Social enterprise innovations that bridge service delivery gaps:  
For instance, the firm Hello Tractor 154 in Nigeria connects producers with providers of agricultural services (e.g., tractor owners) using Internet of Things (IoT) platforms similar to the one Uber uses to connect drivers with passengers. These solutions not only provide farmers with access to machinery, but also connect them to an ecosystem of partners who use the tractor’s data to provide additional services such as financing and crop-specific weather information.

## • Climate technologies such as early warning systems that provide farmers information and advice on when to plant or sell their livestock.  
The FAO Global Information and Early Warning System provides countries tools to monitor crops and food prices and produce analyses and briefs in real time.

## • Digital finance services that can give smallholder farmers who do not have access to traditional financial services access to loans.  
In Kenya, Apollo155 provides optimized bundled services for farmers that include financing, farming inputs, advisory services and insurance. Apollo’s vertically integrated digital approach leverages machine learning and satellite data to service customers throughout the entire lifecycle, from acquisition to decisions on credit and finally, loan payments.

## • Open data and geo-mapping for improving nature resource planning and monitoring in countries.  
Tools such as Open Foris, SEPAL 156 and trends.earth157 provide digital technology and satellite imagery to support countries in assessing, monitoring and planning land use, including the monitoring of deforestation and desertification. The technology offers wide coverage, is faster than ground methods and facilitates long-term monitoring of land coverage and usage.

## • Clean technologies (e.g., solar, wind) that generate sustainable energy.  
The World Bank Lighting Africa programme158 provides electricity through off-grid solar products by working with local distributorships in sub-Saharan Africa.

## • Financial innovations (e.g., crowdfunding, social impact bonds, mobile money).  
FSD Africa has developed an innovative approach to financing mechanisms that drive sustainability in conservation areas in Kenya 159.

## • Innovative adaptation approaches that provide climate smart services to villages.  
CGIAR climate-smart villages160 bring a holistic approach to village development by combining agroforestry, precision application of fertilizers and energy-efficient machinery with other climate-smart services, such as tailored weather forecasts to help farmer’s better plan planting, harvesting and other activities on the farm. These services are delivered through phones which farmers can also use to buy index-based insurance that gives them some protection in the event of extreme weather.

## 162. In the context of the GGW RSP and IGREENFIN I, specific attention will be given to support accredited entities in leveraging opportunities to integrate precision farming technologies in projects in view of the potential they have to boost value chain resilience and contribute to landscape restoration.  
Precision farming technologies can be defined as a whole-farm management approach that uses information technology, satellite positioning data, remote sensing and proximal data collection for more efficient use of certain inputs and water resources. In the GGW, these technologies will be introduced with the goal of optimizing input usage and reducing environmental impacts, including GHG emissions161.

## Broadly speaking, there are three main components to technology applications:  
(i) hardware and sensors (which are involved in using or collecting data);  
(ii) data analysis and decision support systems (the software and/or cloud systems and machine learning algorithms which gather and process data to turn it into something meaningful), and  
(iii) commodity and whole-farm focus (the process whereby decision support systems and models are made commercially and utilized across whole farms, and not just individual fields).

## Alongside the economic benefits of precision farming, there is also much discussion on the vital role these technologies play in building a climate resilient and sustainable agricultural sector. Several studies note that precision farming enables farmers to maintain or increase productivity/y

### Key activities for Output 3.2 include:  

#### Activity 3.2.1 Build a digital and innovation ecosystem  
A digital and innovation ecosystem is the evolving set of actors, activities, artifacts, institutions and relations (including complementary and substitute relations) that are important for the adoption of digital technologies that facilitate multi-actor interaction and contribute to the development of innovative climate adaptability solutions that can be deployed at scale. Such an ecosystem fosters economic stability and promotes resource sharing for the development of innovation and technologies and the creation of new market opportunities.  

Considering the challenges in the GGW, the RSP will act as a broker of the digital ecosystem by promoting collaboration among different actors, including government(s), towards the achievement of key objectives of the GGW . It will do so by:  

- Mapping existing services and digital platforms that provide extension services, financial services, farming precision technologies and digital technologies that help monitor and plan the use of natural resources , among other services. This mapping will include key players in the region and existing platforms and services , including those promoted or implemented by GCF and GEF projects , as well as technologies and platforms developed by MSMEs, entrepreneurs, academia and other players in the region. This mapping will be done at the time of the programme’s inception to allow the RSP to define the best strategy for developing and hosting the platform in the GGW context , which is evolving very rapidly since the One Planet Summit of January 2021. The mapping will also include recommendations for opportunities to leverage these technologies across the pillars of the GGW Accelerator and the promising solutions that can be scaled up – for instance, through GCF support for climate technology incubators and accelerators165.  

- Providing technical support for brokering the relationship between solutions providers participating in the marketplace and GCF, GEF and other partners at country level.  

- Facilitating a series of online events to showcase specific innovations and share expertise across the region, which will be accessible to GCF, GEF and other partners at the country and regional level.  

- Organizing annual fairs based on the model of SahelInnov Expo 166 where climate technologies in specific areas (e.g. farm precision technologies, digital solutions for monitoring and planning restoration, access to financing, etc.) in the GGW region and beyond will be showcased and sessions will be organized to discuss and address specific climate challenges. The fairs’ events will facilitate the interaction of different skills, resources, knowledge or needs by matching interests or creating standardized technological interfaces and communication forums. The events will be complementary to the marketplace by providing opportunities for real-time demonstrations and networking.  

#### Activity 3.2.2 Create a digital marketplace with all services provided by platforms and other providers  
Based on the mapping from activity 3.2.1, an online marketplace will be created to bring together financial and non-financial institutions, civil society organizations, AgTech and FinTech agencies, value chain

## B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

### Organization and staffing of IGREENFIN I

#### IFAD Regional Coordination Units (RCU):

165. In its capacity as AE for IGREENFIN I, IFAD will create a Regional Coordination Unit (RCU) which will oversee, compile, consolidate and control the work of the EEs. The EEs will implement the programme through IFAD baseline investment PMUs (grant proceeds) and the LNABs’ PIUs. The RCU finance team will be established and located in IFAD’s West and Central Africa Hub based in Dakar, Senegal. The RCU’s financial management unit will be composed of one employee: the Finance Officer. IFAD PMU staff will undergo an annual performance evaluation in line with IFAD performance assessment policy and procedures. Refer to Section 1 of Annex 22 for the duties and responsibilities for each AE and EE.

#### National Executing Entities (NEEs):

166. The NEEs for IGREENFIN I are the Republic of Burkina Faso, the Republic of Côte d’Ivoire, the Republic of Ghana, the Republic of Mali and the Republic of Senegal, represented by their respective ministries of economy and finance. The AE will inform each N EE of the relevant obligations and requirements established in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) and Funded Activity Agreement (FAA). The NEEs will be recipients of the GCF financing and guarantee the repayment of the GCF loan portion. The NEEs have a proven track record in similar projects and have been working with IFAD and other financial institutions on rural finance.

#### The selected LNABs responsible for the implementation of green credit lines with the GCF loan proceeds (Component 1) are:

- Agricultural Bank of Burkina Faso
- National Investment Bank of Côte d’Ivoire
- ARB Apex Bank Limited of Ghana
- Agricultural Bank of Mali
- Agricultural Bank of Senegal

The LNABs shall submit all applications for financing under the green credit lines (windows 1 and 2), together with their preliminary assessment and recommendations based on independent expert opinion, to the NEEs. LNABs shall not be entitled or authorized to give the final clearance for a loan or sub-grant application; this decision will be made exclusively by the NEEs. Accordingly, the NEEs will verify whether the application meets the eligibility criteria and decide whether to endorse the LNABs’ recommendations, but the final approval or rejection of a subproject lies with the NEE.

For the implementation of the GCF grant proceeds (Component 2), the NEEs will make arrangement with the Ministries of Agriculture of the respective countries who will act through IFAD PMUs. The executing entities of the GCF grant proceeds for IGREENFIN I are:

- Ministry of Agriculture, Hydro-Agricultural Development and Mechanization of Burkina Faso
- Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development of Côte d’Ivoire
- Ministry of Agriculture of Mali
- Ministry of Agriculture of Ghana
- Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Equipment of Senegal

IFAD has conducted a capacity assessment of the LNABs and the Ministries of Agriculture of the five targeted countries.

#### Regional Steering Committee (RSC):

Given the geographical and thematic scope of the different components, a joint inter-ministerial decision will establish the Regional Steering Committee (RSC) and specify its composition, mandate and functioning. The nomination of members to the RSC

## IFAD Baseline Project Management Units (PMUs):

169. In each of the selected countries, the grant proceeds will be anchored within each IFAD baseline PMU. Each baseline PMU will budget for two additional headcounts to manage IGREENFIN I. Baseline PMU staff working on IGREENFIN I will undergo an annual performance evaluation in line with baseline PMU’s performance assessment policy and procedures.

## LNABs Project Investment Units (PIUs):

170. In each country, the selected LNABs will implement the IGREENFIN I loan proceeds. Each LNAB will set up a dedicated Project Investment Unit (PIU) whose primary role will be to receive the GCF proceeds. The funds will be disbursed to the LNAB through the IFAD baseline PMUs. The LNABs will on-lend to beneficiaries or other IFIs. Financial management activities will be managed using the LNAB’s existing financial human resources and systems in place. Each LNAB will also name a focal point within its structure that will be responsible for all financial management and accounting issues related to the management of GCF funds within the bank. The PIU will work with rural finance institutions (RFIs) to gain better access to target beneficiaries. It is the NEEs’ responsibility to determine how they will work with RFIs. The PIU will also be responsible for conducting a due diligence review of the partner institutions that will be involved in the programme and ensure that mitigating actions are put in place to deal with the key risks identified and ensure that they can respond to all operational and reporting requirements. PIUs will be required to obtain a non-objection letter from IFAD regarding their choice of partner institutions.

## IFAD RCU:
172. The IFAD RCU will carry out an ex-post review of a risk-based sample of Withdrawal Applications (WAs) As but will not be directly involved when Baseline PMUs make WA or Direct Payment requests to IFAD. The ex-post review is to support improvements in the quality of financial management. Any discrepancies noted will be communicated to IFAD and the baseline PMU and recommended actions included in the assurance review tracker for monitoring of implementation.

## IFAD Baseline PMU in the flow of funds:
173. Baseline PMUs will open two new bank accounts to use exclusively to manage IGREENFIN I proceeds, namely a Grant Designated Account and a Loan Designated Account. The Grant Designated Account will be used for payment of project operating costs relating to IGREENFIN I incurred by the baseline project and for the replenishment of Bank PIU overhead costs. IGREENFIN I loan proceeds will be deposited in the baseline project's Loan Designated Account and transferred to the bank against appropriate reporting using the revolving fund mechanism.

## PIU within the LNABs:
174. Disbursement of IGREENFIN I loan proceeds to the LNABS will be based on a revolving funds mechanism. The LNABs will open two IGREENFIN I operating accounts within the bank; one account for the IGREENFIN I loan proceeds and the other account for the IGREENFIN I grant proceeds. The amount of the IGREENFIN I proceeds allocated to each LNABs for the entire life of the programme will be stipulated in the IFAD Financing Agreement. The LNABs PIU will receive an initial deposit amounting to the budget for the first six (6) months of operations for both the loan and grant proceeds, as stipulated in the AWPB.

175. The LNAB's PIU will then transmit further disbursement requests to the Baseline PMU for approval and disbursements on a quarterly basis, justifying the expenditures related to the previous tranches of payment made in the grant and loan proceeds accounts. Apart from the initial deposit, subsequent disbursements will be preceded by the submission of Loan Disbursement/ Repayment Report and supporting documents, which will serve as justification for the previous instalments.

## 182. Support Unit: The Support Unit will be responsible for implementing activities under Component 3 (RSP). - Program Coordinator: overall coordination of Component 3  
- Knowledge Management Specialist: responsible for Output 3.1  
- Innovation Specialist: responsible for Output 3.2

- Administrative Assistant  

### Country level specialists: For each country, IFAD will hire a Knowledge Management Specialist to support the NDAs and GGW Focal Point with the implementation of the Regional Support Programme’s activities. Country specialists will report to the Regional Support Unit’s KM Specialist and will share their time between the NDAs office and the IFAD office to coordination of activities. Country KM Specialists will be supported by the regional knowledge management and policy specialist. They will have a coordinating role for knowledge management, communication and policy in support of the NDAs.

## 183. GGW RSP Liaison Officers: The GGW Accelerator will host a GGW Regional Support Programme liaison officer to ensure close alignment and collaboration with the GGW Accelerator. These liaison functions will include:

b. Coordinating the reporting of GGW RSP activities and progress to the GGW Accelerator to increase the collective impact of the RSP and Accelerator projects and programmes  
c. Ensure alignment and regular feedback between the RSP knowledge hub and the Accelerator GGW knowledge hub on best practices and lessons learned  
d. Liaise with GGW structures, processes and institutions (PAAGGW, AU, and GGW Steering Committee) to ensure they are well informed of the progress of the RSP.

The Country Coordination Committees will also be responsible for the coordination of the activities reported to the GGW Accelerator to ensure transparent and comprehensive exchange of information and reporting. ## B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

193. In the GGW region where markets are still small; the cost of capital, high; credit terms, unfavourable, and the upfront costs of adopting adaptation and mitigation measures are high, GCF financing can help to attract the private sector to participate in green business investment and support the achievement of the GGW’s objectives by 2030. The main barriers hindering investment in green business projects in the GGW region and Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana are limited local financial market capacity and appetite of international institutional investors for green business initiatives. This is mainly due to the perception of green technologies/business models along the targeted agricultural value chains as high-risk investments, but also concerns with the macroeconomic environment, limited credit worthiness of smallholder farmers, country risks, policy support and limited liquidity of national agricultural banks. To close the financing gap, countries need public concessional finance that will leverage additional private sector investment to create a viable green business market in the region. Covid-19 has further impacted investment activity in the five selected countries, as institutional investors - particularly those interested in smallholder agriculture - become all the more risk averse. The West Africa region's growth prospects were much less favourable in the second and third quarters of 2020, which are expected to lead to negative annual growth. According to AfDB, West African countries were expected to enter recession in 2020, with a 3.6 per cent contraction of the regional economy. In 2020, forecasts predicted a sharp deterioration in the current account deficit for ECOWAS as a whole, which was expected to stand at 4.

## 194. Therefore, IFAD has approached the GCF to seek the required “capital protection” to incentivize and de-risk public and private institutional investment and create local and regional green business markets which will attract private sector investments. 203. When it comes to market investment particularity in sub-Saharan Africa, investor confidence is very important. IGREENFIN I will support the establishment of a platform for green development banks/central banks regulators in the region to advance the policy dialogue and coordination with governments and partners on green finance for the SDGs; the NDCs and the Paris Climate Agreements, which can be sustained of supported by other partners after the programme ends. IGREENFIN I is building a network of banks with the highest standards of ESG and setting up an open-source platform (ESMF underwriting procedures), which eventually will help attract and guide other prospective players in the market. ## D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA  

This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.

### D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

213. IGREENFIN I will support the greening of the financial system in the five selected countries by building the capacity of five local national agricultural banks to offer green financing for climate resilient, low emission agriculture projects (green governance and lending products, green credit indicator system for management, long-term loan periods and unlocking barriers to innovative climate finance). It will also support 1,500 MSMEs and 2,500 FOs and cooperatives, as well as the transfer or licensing of 200 technologies and innovative solutions for climate resilient, low-emission agricultural development. Its interventions will contribute to reducing the targeted countries’ GHG emissions by 5.6 million tCO2 eq over the lifetime of the programme. The adaptation benefits of this programme will reach 378,600 direct and 2,494,000 indirect beneficiaries, of which 50 per cent will be women, 50 per cent men (50 per cent youth and 50 per cent adult males). It will build the capacity of the Agricultural Bank of Senegal and the Centre de Suivi Ecologique (CSE), and other four LNABs’ readiness and ability to mobilize additional concessional finance from both the private and public sector.

214. Innovative green business models including climate resistant breeds and crop varieties, sustainable land restoration techniques, solar energy to power drip irrigation and processing, packaging and storage equipment, among others, for the ten selected value chains provide an indication of how EUR 177,481,678 million in funds for IGREENFIN I (i.e., GCF resources plus additional resources mobilized) can be used to produce positive impacts and ensure both financial and economic returns on investment. The green business could be scaled up by the financial sector in each of the targeted countries and in the GGW region.

215. IGREENFIN I will contribute to the implementation of the GGWI, which aims to restore 100 million hectares of currently degraded land, sequester 250 million tons of carbon and create 10 million jobs in rural areas by 2030. Spanning the entire width of Africa – 7,775 km from Senegal to Djibouti – the “Wall” is a mosaic of projects implemented by countries in the region with the support of development partners as a pan-African sustainable landscape programme to address land degradation and desertification, boost food, environmental and economic security and support the adaptation of communities to climate change. It aims to do much more than just grow trees: its goals are to i) foster climate resilience for local communities in a region set to be hit by a 3 to 5˚C temperature increase by 2050; ii) safeguard food security for the 20 million people in the Sahel that go hungry every year; iii) support the creation of “green” jobs to provide stable incomes for African women and youth; iv) build a global response to the migration crisis, with EUR 49,314 million people set to migrate to Europe from degraded parts of Africa by 2021, and v) grow peace and security in the Sahel, where conflict has already displaced 2.8 million people so far in 2021. Through IGREENFIN I, IGREENFIN II and the Regional Support Programme, this programme will serve as the investment pillar of the future GCF -GGW RSP, which is supported by UNCCD and the 3S Initiative, two major African-led initiatives set up with the bold ambition to restore the productivity and vitality of the Sahel region, while ‘growing solutions’ to the continent’s most urgent development challenges. IGREENFIN I Phase 1 will channel resources to beneficiaries and provide crucial funds to help accelerate the implementation of the GGW, which began in 2007. It will also contribute to advances on the African Union’s Agenda 2063 in the region.

216. Climate impacts will be monitored at the programme level using the core GCF mitigation/adaptation indicators, consistent with the GCF results management and performance measurement frameworks. The tool to track these indicators will be defined in cooperation with the LNABs, IFAD baseline investments and in accordance with the green business plans that will be developed by FOs, MSMEs, cooperatives and targeted sectors through the credit line. The final impacts will be measured by an ex-post impact assessment

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 89 OF 116

## Programme Implementation Approach

The Regional Support Programme will be implemented using a phased approach:

- **Phase 0**: The Simplified Approval Process IGREENFIN I project in Niger (SAP012) approved by the GCF Board in November 2019.
- **Phase 1** will be implemented in the first set of three GGW countries (Burkina Faso, Mali and Senegal) plus Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana
- **Phase 2** will be implemented in the second set of seven GGW countries ( Chad, Djibouti, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Mauritania, Nigeria and Sudan)

## IGREENFIN I Objectives

IGREENFIN I aims to engage national agricultural banks, national banking associations, banking regulators, green standards settings bodies, local universities and business schools on green finance in agriculture. Through the mainstreaming work, these institutions will be able to sustain the GCF grant and concessionality beyond the programme's duration.

The programme will support the production of studies and policy papers on climate risk management to define the standards, develop and harmonize green lending products, instruments and tools such as ESG benchmarks. It will also build knowledge on green finance invested in the agricultural sector by both the private sector and governments.

IGREENFIN I will work closely with the national agricultural banks to develop to improve their ESG, international capacity and better align part of their lending portfolio on the NDCs. By building the banks' capacity to offer affordable green finance for smallholder agriculture, the programme is starting to fill the gap in financing for agriculture and alter the perception smallholder from a risky business to a vast market with great potential and opportunities.

## Green Credit Lines and Awareness Campaigns

Green credit lines and awareness campaigns will support the development of demand for green products, particularly for youth and women, and concrete viable pipelines of green business projects, reduce the financial sector's perception of smallholders as high risk and hence, the development of new green markets in sectors that have been traditionally difficult to reach (for example, the MSME sector). Beneficiaries such as MSMEs will benefit from training activities and affordable finance (available from local financial institutions) and techniques that help them reduce their operating costs while increasing their productivity and incomes.

The early stages of the programme's operations are expected to demonstrate to end users the effectiveness and value of the solutions proposed, which will stimulate demand beyond the target financial institutions and target countries to other market sectors and areas in the GGW after the programme ends. This programme will facilitate the transition towards granted funded rural financial projects to sustainable long term markets rural finance projects.

## Potential for Knowledge Sharing and Learning

Markets for climate change adaptation and mitigation solutions, particularly for small and medium-sized projects in the GGW countries and the Sahel, remain underdeveloped and thus require support to develop at the pace needed to achieve national emission reduction targets. IGREENFIN I is designed to foster the development of new markets for green finance and climate resilient, low emission agriculture in the GGW areas, contribute to overcoming barriers and create a long-term enabling environment for investments in these sectors.

IGREENFIN I will put in place long-term robust structures for green lending operated by five LNABs in the five countries of the GGW during the programme's first phase. The green credit lines to be created under component 1, when combined with the capacity-building on the development of green business plans offered by the TAF to FOs, MSMEs, cooperatives and women and youth-led organisations, will help reduce the risks of both lending and borrowing in the sector, thus contributing to even more robust long-term impacts and sustainable market growth while aligning part of the LNABs portfolio on the country's NDCs.

## Contribution to the Regulatory Framework and Policies

In addition to inadequate legal frameworks to systematically drive investment in climate change projects, the element that is missing is accessible and affordable financing, which this programme is designed to address through the establishment of green finance market. As outlined above, the programme is well-aligned with national emission reduction targets and priorities. Successful performance of the programme and the creation of an enabling market environment will encourage national and local authorities to prioritize strong policies in these sectors.

To address any remaining barriers in the market, IGREENFIN I will continue to carry out policy dialogue activities through the central banks, LNABs and other partners. This programme will also build upon and complement nascent agreements focusing on greening the financial sector in the region. In addition to all the knowledge built, studies, trainings and monitoring of climate impacts carried out throughout the programme cycle by the TAF and the support it will offer for aligning national banks' portfolios with the NDCs, IGREENFIN I will also support the implementation of countries' Climate Investment Plan and NAPs.

## 222. The GCF GGW RSP introduces a new approach piloted by IFAD and the GCF, which aims to catalyse impact beyond a one-off project or programme investment by creating bridges for knowledge sharing and learning between GCF projects that would otherwise be implemented in silos, and among AEs, GCF NDAs, GGW and GEF focal points. This innovative approach has potential for scale up in Africa beyond the GGW countries and could also be replicated in other large landscapes and ecosystems such as the Amazon or in countries with relatively similar needs, such as some SIDS. This UP will also contribute to strengthening collaboration at the country level between the GCF, the GEF and the AF, and bring to life a concrete example of complementarity and collaboration between the UNFCCC and the UNCCD.

## D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 223. IGREENFIN I and the Regional Support Programme have identified and adopted the following UN SDGs and mapped each of the target sectors to them: No poverty (SDG 1); Zero hunger (SDG 2); Gender equality (SDG 5); Clean water and sanitation (SDG 6); Affordable and clean energy (SDG 7); Decent work and economic growth (SDG 8); Climate action (SDG 13); Life on land (SDG 15), and Partnerships for the Goals (SDG 17).

### 224. Environmental co-benefits: The programme is designed to help secure multiple environmental benefits for water, air, land and nature by promoting the adoption of best adaptation and mitigation practices and technology, which will enhance the sustainable management of natural resources, protect threatened dryland biodiversity against erosion and desertification and increase the potential for carbon sequestration. These benefits could also contribute to the increased resilience of the region’s ecosystems and human livelihoods to climate change and variability and the GGW’s overall objectives (land restoration, job creation and CO2 emission reduction). The programme intends to reduce the use of energy from biomass, including firewood and agricultural residues, imported diesel fuel and charcoal, which are the main sources of electricity in the five targeted countries in IGREENFIN I, by providing greater access to renewable energy. It is expected that CO2 emissions (5.6 million tCO2 eq) will be reduced during the programme’s lifecycle thanks to agroforestry and SLM practices (this amount will further increase once the avoided emission from RET is integrated) and limits will be placed on the use of biomass and diesel for energy consumption through the adoption of RET. With the promotion of EbA and agroforestry techniques, more than 100,000 ha of degraded land will be restored and sustainably managed with improved ecosystem goods and services. The programme will build up each link in the selected agricultural value chains (including livestock and NTFPs) from production to markets, increasing the productivity for key commodities and farmers’ incomes while safeguarding the environment. Through the adoption of climate resilient agriculture, the programme will reduce soil erosion and soil fertility loss, enhance soil moisture retention and maintain soil temperatures suitable for crop production.

### 225. Social co-benefits including health impacts: The activities proposed under this programme will generate multiple job opportunities for rural people, particularly for youth and women, promote inclusion and support coherent investment through FOs, cooperatives and MSMEs. By promoting local climate resilient, low emission options, the programme will contribute to cost-saving health benefits thanks to cleaner air for local communities and access to clean energy, water and food, thus reducing food insecurity. As described in the gender action analysis and action plan, the programme will support women’s participation in decision-making, increase their access to financing and assets and improve their livelihoods. The targeted areas considered the most vulnerable and selected through IFAD baseline investments will be positively impacted by the programme.

### 226. Economic co-benefits: The economic benefits of this programme are linked to its high potential to reduce poverty through increased productivity of key crops and livestock production, as well as income generation activities (IGA), such as the production of and value addition to different fruit and NTFPs, including tamarind, Arabic gum, baobab leaves as well as fodder and seedlings sales. The GGW implementation status report indicated that revenues from IGA since 2007 have amounted to approximately EUR73,971 million across all 11 GGWI countries, with Burkina Faso being more successful in creating IGA opportunities than the others. Revenues reported per country are as follow: Burkina Faso, US$6.8 million, and US$50,000 for Mali and Senegal each.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 91 OF 116

## Change in the world

The programme will contribute to create 703,600 jobs. By doing so, the programme provides a unique opportunity to put an end to poverty traps.

## Gender-sensitive development impacts

Women are increasingly and disproportionately affected by climate change. In the five participating countries, women play a central role in feeding their families and ensuring other basic needs (water, fuel for firewood, etc.). This responsibility has become harder to assume because of declining crop yields due to climate change (primarily higher temperatures and unpredictable rainfall patterns). The programme will increase opportunities for livelihood improvement and provide concrete benefits to smallholder farmers and pastoralists, both men and women. The implementation of targeted activities can potentially offer an array of advantages to local communities, such as greater yields, improved soil fertility, fodder availability, as well as shorter wood and water collection time for women, freeing up their time so they may engage in other productive tasks.

Through its focus on gender mainstreaming and women’s economic empowerment, this programme will support the reduction of entrenched gender inequalities in rights to land resources and access to technology and information that hamper women’s capacity to manage current climate risks and adapt their livelihoods to long-term climate change trends. The programme has set targets: 50 per cent of loans are to be granted to women-led MSMEs, cooperatives and FOs. The other 50 per cent will be dedicated to men of which 50% will be youth-led MSMEs, cooperatives and FOs in which young women participate. Specific actions will be developed to strengthen the technical and managerial capacities of women aimed at providing them with appropriate tools for identifying and developing bankable business plans and improving their level of financial education.

As stated in the gender action plan, gender-disaggregated data will be assessed against the appropriate indicator to measure women’s enhanced access to loans. The project intends to close the gender gap, as women represent 60 to 70 per cent of the workforce and do not have access to productive assets, finance and knowledge. At least 1/3 of the women will be included into the National Steering Committee to ensure that they influence the main strategic decisions.

## Needs of recipients (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Participating countries in this programme are highly vulnerable to drought, flooding, rising temperatures and variability in rainfall, as well as other climate change hazards (wildfires, locust infestations, etc.). Their exposure and vulnerability to climate hazards is projected to increase in the future. Climate projections suggest that the West Sahel and northern parts of Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana will be hotter and drier with more frequent extreme events. In most scenarios, temperature rise will accelerate, meaning that the continent on average could be between 2 and 6°C warmer by end of this century (ACMAD, 2020).

Climate impacts have negative impacts on agriculture. They reduce productivity, food security and GDP, cause endemic diseases (malaria) and increase rural poverty. Given the five countries’ high poverty rate (see feasibility study for more details) and the impacts of climate change, the country has few financial institutions that want to risk investing in smallholder farming despite the fact that agriculture remains the main source of economic growth.

The five countries’ financial system remains weak and has a limited number of instruments for financing agriculture. LNABs have limited possibilities of mobilizing highly concessional loans in the capital markets. FOs and MSMEs, cooperatives, youth and women-led organizations active in the agricultural sector often lack access to long-term financing with affordable conditions (high interest rates, tenors and grace periods) due to small loan sizes and relatively high transaction costs. Key barriers are limited adaptation capacity to climate change across the country, lack of skills and knowledge on climate finance and climate resilient, low emission agriculture, the nascent regulatory framework for these sectors and the need to de-risk the sector.

Technical assistance and funding for the establishment of a Green Financing Facility that will provide concessional loans and climate smart credit risk tools will enhance access to credit, contribute to de-risking the sector and foster greater knowledge on climate finance and adaptation across the selected countries. They will directly help overcome the key barriers that LNABs face in rural finance and enable them to engage in green financing.

This programme will contribute to the implementation of objectives of the three Rio conventions ratified by all countries, including the UNFCCC, as well as the Paris Climate Agreement, the SDGs and the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction.

## 236. Feasibility studies and market analyses were conducted on agricultural value chains and sectors relevant to this programme to assess both demand and supply and the need to stimulate and create a new market for green business projects (see Annex 2 Feasibility Study, Market Assessment, Renewable energy report).

## 237. In 2020, IFAD was proposed to lead the Regional Support Programme of the GGF. IFAD will work in partnership with other agencies including UNCCD, FAO, GEF and WB on the GCF GGW RSP. IGREENFIN I will be one of the financing programmes under the future GGW RSP. Coordination with the GGW will help leverage additional resources and capacities.

## 238. The GGW RSP has been developed in response to a call from the GGW countries for the GCF and the international community to increase the level of support to accelerate the pace of the implementation of the activities contributing to the achievement of the GGW’s objectives. It is fully aligned with the principle of country ownership and it respects, and builds on, the institutional structure already in place to support the implementation of the GGW Initiative. The GGW RSP is aligned with GGW Accelerator, which is led by the GGW Agency with the temporary support of the UNCCD. At the country level, the GGW RSP aims to enhance the capacities of the GCF NDAs, as well as of the GEF and AF focal points, by hiring GIS and KM specialists to help them during the planning of future GCF, GEF and AF projects and their implementation through enhanced KM exchanges.

## D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## 239. In the GGW region, green businesses are by and large financially underserved in terms of the availability of both highly concessional loans from LNABs, commercial banks and MFIs and technical assistance. Section B3 describes how GCF resources will be used efficiently under interlinked activities to help overcome the barriers identified, including the approach to ensure minimum concessionality. Activities under IGREENFIN I aim to fill a financing gap by providing financial and non-financial support. In doing so, the programme will jump start a nascent market that is currently far from reaching its full potential. As this market develops over time, opportunities will be created to attract other public and private sector investors in these LDCs (Burkina Faso, Mali and Senegal) and the two LMICs (Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana) that have all the characteristics of LDCs, namely high levels of poverty and vulnerability to climate change.

## 240. IGREENFIN I will nurture and scale up all the green financing products and green business models it generates to other relevant projects underway in the targeted countries and regions. By building synergies and leveraging IGREENFIN I resources, the programme and other entities will adequately reach the maximum beneficiaries. Partnerships with LNABs and EEs will be promoted through the provision of green loans and technical assistance to de-risk all potential subprojects. GCF financing will be essential to overcome existing barriers to access to financial and non-financial services that hinder the adoption and implementation of best climate change adaptation and mitigation measures (including solar energy for agriculture, among others). Additionally, even though LNABs have been performing quite well over the past years with various loan products, their loans are still not concessional enough to stimulate greater adherence to climate resilient, low emission agriculture and generate a strong green business pipeline. In the energy sector, several bilateral donors have put in place concessional financing schemes that combine grants with credit to promote agricultural and rural finance; they work mainly through MFIs and state-linked financial institutions, such as the targeted LNABs. Early results from these programmes are highly encouraging, although stronger oversight and technical assistance (TA) are still needed. This new approach to transform the LNABs’ internal governance and set up a facility with green finance windows is considered innovative and transformational in the context of climate change. From an effectiveness point of view, IGREENFIN I is projected to achieve a leverage ratio of 1: 0.7 (i.e., EU0.57 of additional public and private funding leveraged for every EUR 0, 82 of GCF investment); thus, a EUR104, 134 million investment from the GCF is expected to leverage about EUR 73,971 million in additional public and private contributions (including co-financing as per Section C.1.b).

## 242. Regarding adaptation, 10 value chains for each country were analyzed with estimated the economic and financial performance of the investment for different types of local stakeholders. The IRR for the various VCs is as follows:  
- VC1 (20%)  
- VC2 (40%)  
- VC3 (55%)  
- VC4 (70%)  
- VC5 (10%)  
- VC6 (60%)  
- VC7 (80%)  
- VC8 (40%)  
- VC9 (30%)  
- VC10 (50%)  

With payback time in the range of one to five years. When we consider country performance, the IRR ranges from 37 per cent for Mali to 51 per cent for Côte d’Ivoire. Finally, when considering different beneficiaries, the highest IRR is found for MSMEs in Burkina Faso and Ghana (over 80 per cent), for cooperatives in Côte d’Ivoire (47 per cent), Mali (68 per cent) and Senegal (52 per cent). The IRR for farmer organizations is generally the lowest in all countries, ranging from 24 to 35 per cent.

## 243. The GCF contribution will be very important to both improve the financial performance of the investment (i.e., by lowering the interest payment) and create a buffer for less productive years (e.g., sensitivity analysis shows that a 20 per cent decline in revenues or a similar increase in production costs will result in negative IRR and NPV for several VCs).

## 244. When taking a societal approach that considers the economic valuation of externalities (e.g., carbon and other ecosystem services), including those that do not generate a cash flow, the IRR increases to 100 per cent for adaptation. This highlights the value of the GCF contribution beyond the direct economic impacts of the investments proposed, resulting in a net positive contribution to society.

## 245. Regarding mitigation, the IRR and NPV are positive, and the debt coverage ratio (DCRE) is above one for energy investors in mini grids when considering the price of $0.61 - $0.87/kWh. This shows that mini grids are more financially viable than bringing centralized power generation to rural areas and using fully decentralized diesel power generators. It is worth noting that with any price lower than $0.8 – $1/kWh, farmers would also benefit from cost savings, thus making the investment advantageous for all economic actors. The IRR is 25 per cent (with a price of $0.87/kWh) and declines to 14 per cent (with a price of $0.61/kWh). The GCF contribution makes the DCR more desirable, increasing from 1.9 to 2.57 (with a price of $0.87/kWh) or increasing from 1.39 to 1.83 (with a price of $0.61/kWh).

## 246. With regard to farmers as investors/beneficiaries: If a farming cooperative were to invest in a mini grid, it would enable them to have access to affordable electricity and minimize spending on energy. The price that would allow the mini grid investment to be economically viable is $0.4/kWh. This is the minimum price that would justify investing in a mini grid (i.e., generating a positive IRR and NPV). Furthermore, this price would allow consumers (farmers and those working in the food value chain) to save $0.5/kWh consumed when compared to using diesel generators. If we compare the investment to cost savings, the IRR reaches 11 per cent and the NPV is positive for all countries. In other words, the savings are large enough to reduce the IRR to a value that is comparable to the interest rates requested by local national agricultural banks, and twice the rate requested by commercial banks. In this respect, the contribution of IGREENFIN I and GCF is essential. In fact, while the IRR and NPV are positive, the debt coverage ratio (DCR) is smaller than 1 when considering bank rates. When using the preferential rates guaranteed by the IGREENFIN I instead, the DCR is above 1, indicating economic viability for the programme considering all IRR, NPV and DCR. Further, if we carry out a more systemic analysis and consider indirect benefits and reduction of externalities, the IRR grows to a range of 20 to 40 per cent, depending on the scenario.

## E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Reporting from IFAD to GCF

Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements will comply with IFAD’s Accreditation Master Agreement and GCF policies, as well as the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA). IFAD through the RCU will provide annual progress reports on the status of the funded activity throughout the relevant reporting period, based on the above-described logical framework and reporting from IFAD baseline investments to IFAD RCU. The RCU will consolidate above described annual, mid-term and final reports from each country IGREENFIN I project submitted by IFAD baseline investments and send them to the GCF within additional thirty (30) days of the above-mentioned reporting periods by the IFAD.

IFAD will deliver numbers in a log frame format:

a) annually within sixty (60) calendar days after the end of each year of programme implementation;

b) at mid-term within thirty (30) months from the effective date of the loan contract or when 50 per cent of the programme resources have been disbursed, whichever occurs first;

c) at programme end up to six (6) months after the day of the last disbursement to a participating country and its banks.

### IGREENFIN I monitoring and reporting

IGREENFIN I will apply the standard procedures established by IFAD for monitoring and evaluating investment operations, including co-financing (IFAD, ISDB, and AfDB). The IFAD RCU will compile and maintain all information, indicators and parameters necessary for the preparation of programme reports, including annual reports, midterm review and final evaluation to be submitted to the GCF.

The agricultural banks and selected partners (commercial banks, MFIs) will be responsible for assessing beneficiaries’ business plans in accordance with the programme’s eligibility criteria, as defined in the Annex 23: GCF Investment Criteria for sub loans. Business plan approved by the selected financial institutions must be properly identified in the banks’ accounting systems and be in compliance with what is stated in the specific loan agreements. These records should allow for identifying financial conditions of each transaction (e.g., currency, maturity and interest rates), the value of the contract, loan proceeds and eventual use of proceeds for monitoring purposes, programme funds balances and default rates, if necessary.

The monitoring process intends to follow up on the execution of the programme in order to identify the intermediate milestones achieved in each phase and evaluate its outcomes and fulfilment of proposed targets. The indicators to be monitored are included in the log frame in section E.

#### Description  

250. ESG reporting: The IFAD RCU will receive an annual report on the ESG performance of the national agricultural banks. The PIU teams will take inputs from relevant teams and compile the annual report for each applicable reporting period. Annual climate impact reporting will be performed by the banks, while the independent mid-term and final year verification of climate impact will be undertaken by an independent provider.  

251. GHG reporting: ICRAF, a strategic partner of IGREENFIN I, and the Regional Support Programme will be in charge of the reporting on GHG emissions in line with the methodology in the EXCAT Annex.  

#### Regional Support Programme impact assessments:  

252. Impact evaluations are a rigorous tool to assess the contribution and impact of programmes and to improve the next-generation GCF programmes that can benefit from the best evidence-based design options available. The impact assessment of the Regional Support Programme will focus on the 11 countries. It will take an ex-ante/ex-post approach to assess the contribution of the Regional Support Programme’s activities to the paradigm shift. It will entail the development of a questionnaire to define the outcome variables before the Regional Support Programme starts. A baseline will be established at the beginning of the implementation of the Regional Support Programme and the same questionnaire will be submitted upon completion to the beneficiaries, namely the 11 countries’ NDAs and GGW Focal Points as well as the Accredited Entities.  

#### Monitoring and evaluation in the context of the GCF GGW RSP  

253. The Regional Support Programme will not substitute the AEs during the M&E process. Each AE remains accountable for the elaboration and submission of the Annual Performance Reports of its own projects to the GCF Secretariat. FAO will provide additional support through the SURAGWA project to the AEs and the NDAs to help them understand which means of verifications will be used by the GGW Accelerator, as they can also be used by the individual GCF projects directly.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 109 OF 116

## 256. Credit default and end beneficiaries' failure to repay the loans and meet their contractual obligations

Credits risks are inherent to banking activities and can pose a challenge to banks performance.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

257. The credits are administrated by credible financial institutions with excellent knowledge of their customers and the market within which they operate. IFAD has assessed each bank’s financial management capacities. The selected banks internal system will ensure a proper assessment of loans based on investment criteria, including creditworthiness and other qualitative criteria deemed necessary. The bank policy and criteria for credit assessments and measures will safeguard and limit the risks. In addition, the GCF proceeds to support the loans programme are all guaranteed by the governments of the five participating countries that will act as a borrower of last resort.

## Selected Risk Factor 2  
### Category: Probability Impact  
Forex  
Low  
Description  

258. Recipient countries experience currency devaluation or currency fluctuation linked to external shocks. Foreign exchange (FX) risk (or currency risk) exists in financial transactions under Component 1, as IFAD loans are denominated in Euros for the WAEMU zone and loans to final borrowers are likely to be disbursed in a currency other than USD (i.e., the local currency in each country), especially in the case of smaller firms for which transactions occur mainly in local currency. Thus, FX risk arises from the possibility of unfavourable fluctuations between the local currency and the Euros before the loan repayment is completed, as the associated value may decrease due to changes in the relative value of the involved currencies. The XOF currency is pegged to Euros and there won’t be any loss. However, for Ghana which has its own currency called Ghana Cedis, the country is generally prone to undergo sporadic unstable economic conditions, but NEEs generally have the capacity to hedge this risk (i.e., place Euros in the market and manage profits to increase their capacity to offer concessional terms in local currency).

### Mitigation Measure(s)

259. For Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Mali and Senegal: These countries are part of the West African Economic and Monetary Union (WAEMU) which uses the FCFA as their currency (Ghana is the exception and will require more specific measures, as explained below). Owing to the FCFA's fixed exchange rate to the euro, which is currently established at EUR 1 = CFA 655.957, there is no immediate (short-term) foreign exchange risks for these four countries as the GCF proceeds will be denominated in euros. The latest devaluation occurred in 1994, which illustrates the stability of the FCFA, which is also supported by the good performance of WAEMU member’s states. Devaluation or re-evaluation of the FCFA in the long term is a possibility in view of the ongoing discussion on the reform of the FCFA. If such risks should materialize, the WAEMU zone is well-known as a strong monetary zone with its members’ countries positive GDP growth projections, which have however been affected by the COVID–19 pandemic. WAEMU monetary performance is supported by its strong monetary policy, liquidity buffer and continuous fiscal and macroeconomic consolidation efforts by member countries.

260. Ghana: The grant proceeds will be allocated to IFAD which has strong credit risk management, including hedging strategies to reduce exchange rate risks. In addition, Ghana is hedged against forex risk. The Bank of Ghana regulates the conduct of the interbank foreign exchange business in the Ghanaian financial market and establishes and regularly updates standards of practice expected of market participants to help ensure an efficient and effective FX market with the view to continue to develop and deepen the foreign exchange market in Ghana. The Bank of Ghana publish in 2019 its latest interbank forex market conduct and guidelines. As Green business exposed to FX risk, hedging strategies to mitigate it usually involve mechanisms such as forward contracts, options and others, which can protect them from exchange rate fluctuations. Capacity building and proper implementation of such mechanisms will be part of the TC activities under Component

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 110 OF 116

## Description  
261. Governance systems remain weak and can pose a key challenge in all the countries included in this proposal.

## Mitigation Measure(s)  
262. IFAD’s presence and engagement at the country level, the use of IFAD PMU and risk management framework, policy dialogue and partnerships will help countries manage this risk. The programme is designed grounded on a robust management arrangement using existing IFAD and countries project governance management systems. Additionally, the programme will establish a regional coordination programme to support its overall implementation and good performance. During its lifecycle, measures will be taken to ensure that the outcomes are sustained beyond the programme lifecycle. With regards to corruption, the programme will implement an absolute zero tolerance to corruption in accordance with IFAD’s internal policy; regular audits and supervision missions will be undertaken.

## Selected Risk Factor 4  
### Category: Probability Impact  
#### Technical and operational  
#### Technical and operational  
#### Technical and operational  

### Description  
263. Sustainable finance practices and integration of climate risks management into banking operations is new for all the countries involved in this programme. Moreover, neither the banks, their clients nor regulators are accustomed to the new products to be developed under this programme.

### Mitigation Measure(s)  
264. The programme results from a demand-driven process. IFAD has initiated this programme upon confirmation of the interest and willingness of national development banks, smallholder farmers and governments during the design phase. The consultation processes occurred as part of the design to understand the need of the beneficiaries. Further consultations and policy dialogue interventions are planned during the programme implementation phase. The implementation of a participatory and inclusive approach will facilitate social mobilization and help ensure the adherence of the beneficiaries. Policy dialogue between public and private actors will be promoted to mitigate the risks.

265. Proper due diligence process led by IFAD and the national executing entities, the local FIs’ financial experts, technical experts from the ministries of agriculture and the oversight of the respective ministries of finance and regulators (the two central banks – Western African Countries Central Bank and the Central Bank of Ghana) will all contribute to ensuring robust engineering, procurement, and governance systems. Additionally, the agreements between the recipient governments and their respective relevant sector ministries are to ensure that they better manage the loans during and beyond the project lifecycle. Programme operation risk will be mitigated by a thorough due diligence process led by IFAD technical and financial experts and by ensuring a robust M&E system is set up and fully operational. TA agreements and procurement procedures will be established at each EE level to support and ensure the high delivery quality outcomes.

266. The IFAD grievance mechanism will be used for the reporting of complaints or allegations of wrongdoing within the projects or activities (whistle blower programme). The description of the IFAD grievance mechanism is presented in the ESMF. The programme will establish a community engagement process and provide access to information on a regular basis. The objective of the IFAD Complaints Procedure is to ensure that appropriate mechanisms are in place to allow individuals and communities to contact IFAD directly and file a complaint if they believe they are or might be adversely affected by an IFAD-funded project/programme that does not comply with IFAD's Social and Environmental Policies and mandatory aspects of SECAP. Complaints must concern environmental, social and climate issues only and should not be accusations of fraudulent or corrupt activities in relation to programme implementation; these are dealt with by IFAD's Office of Audit and Oversight.

267. Eligibility criteria according to IFAD’s grievance mechanism:  
To file a complaint for alleged non-compliance with IFAD's social and environmental policies and mandatory aspects of its SECAP, IFAD will consider only complaints meeting the following criteria:

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 111 OF 116

## Complaints

• The complainants claim that IFAD has failed to apply its social and environmental policies and/or the mandatory provisions set out in SECAP and Safeguards.  
• The complainants claim that they have been or will be adversely affected by IFAD's failure to apply these policies.  

Complaints must be put forward by at least two people who are both nationals of the country concerned and/or living in the programme area. Complaints from foreign locations or anonymous complaints will not be taken into account.  

Complaints must concern projects/programmes currently under design or implementation. Complaints concerning closed projects, or those that are more than 95 per cent disbursed, will not be considered.

## Process According to IFAD’s Grievance Mechanism

Complainants should first bring the matter to the attention of the government or non-governmental organisation responsible for planning or executing the project or programme (ministry of agriculture implementing agency and the ministry of economy and finance and the environmental protection agencies involved in this programme that have with the responsibility for overseeing the work on the field). If the Implementing Agency does not adequately respond, then the matter may be brought to the attention of IFAD. The issue may be brought straight to IFAD if the complainants feel they might be subject to retaliation if they went to the Lead Agency directly.

## How to Submit a Complaint

A complaint relating to non-compliance with IFAD’s Social and Environmental Policies and mandatory aspects of its SECAP can be submitted in any of the following ways:  

• Download the complaints form (Word) through IFAD website: https://www.ifad.org/en/accountability-and-complaints-procedures  
• Send an email to SECAPcomplaints@ifad.org  

In addition, GCF sub projects will as much as possible utilize every available grievance redress mechanism including associations (including farmers’ associations/organizations), traditional council (Paramount Chiefs and elders), village square engagement (consisting of representatives of men, women, and social groups), village general assembly, the project NCPU, etc.

As lending operations being considered in the context of this Programme will be part of financial intermediation schemes in local and international markets, a very low risk is foreseen on having unidentified financing of these practices  

### Mitigation Measure(s)

All banks are regulated by the Central banks: the Central Bank of the WAEMU for Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Mali and Senegal and for Ghana, the Central Bank of Ghana. Because all financial operations under Programme activities are carried out in the context of the financial and capital markets, subject to national regulation, this risk is considered low. Nonetheless, the IFAD will not only establish specific rules and eligibility criteria for all financial transactions in each target country (as part of IFAD project-cycle), but all portfolios financed will be subject to IFAD’s non-objection through NOTUS online tool

# G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

277. IFAD has a strong working relationship with LNABs across Africa and works together with them and project developers to overcome key barriers to financing sustainable investments. IFAD SECAP is the key tool used to promote and enhance the management of environmental and social impacts in the financial sector, transfer knowledge and promote good practices in environmental and social governance (ESG) to strengthen the adoption of ESG principles by rural institutions. Such experience has been instrumental for the design and elaboration of the proposed programme and it’s ESMF, ensuring that appropriate and robust procedures and safeguards are in place for implementing climate change projects in the energy and land and forest use sectors. The ESMF and screening of subprojects by the LNABs will ensure that comprehensive and robust environmental and social screening and management systems are in place, and programme activities and investments and their social and environmental impacts are closely monitored, evaluated and reported on.

278. During the due diligence phase of the LNABs, IFAD will assess the ESMS of each institution and ensure that it is aligned with the programme's ESMF, including the Environmental and Social Safeguards of the banks and the GCF. Participating LNABs will receive support to develop effective environmental and social policies, assess environmental and social risks and categorize operations while evaluating the environmental and social risk of their portfolios. The measures to be applied are:

- Verify that the operations being financed are not prohibited by law.
- Verify that the operations being financed are not included in the programme's List of exclusions in the ESMF.
- Enforce compliance with national legislation related to permits and environmental licenses, use of natural resources and activities with dangerous substances.
- Categorize and evaluate the environmental and social risk of the operations to be financed.
- Verify that the amounts requested by their clients include the implementation of the measures to minimize the environmental and social risks of their operations.
- Present reports on the environmental and social management of its operations according to the frequency established in the credit agreement, which will be fixed according to the risks of its portfolio and the magnitude of the credit granted by the LNABs or it partners.

279. When ESMS systems are either not in place or are weak, IGREENFIN I's TAF will provide technical assistance to help establish or improve such systems and, where possible, support LNABs in improving their ESMS to be able to participate in the programme. All sub loans will be screened by LNABs for their environmental and social risks and impacts prior to credit approval. During this screening phase, projects will be revised according to the LNABs Environmental and Social Risk Analysis and Management System to ensure they adhere to local regulations and laws, including ESIA requirements. The programme will not finance category A or high-risk projects, nor any investments described in the programme exclusion list in annex 23 of this proposal. The programme will be limited to category B projects. As such, it is qualified as I-2. Where necessary, environmental, and social management plans (ESMPs) will be either in place or developed at the final loan beneficiary level to manage identified risks and adverse impacts.

## G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

280. IGREENFIN I will promote gender equality and women's empowerment both at the fund- and project-level. As presented in the Gender Analysis and Action Plan, IGREENFIN I will support the use of gender-disaggregated indicator systems, requiring participating LNABs (agricultural banks), commercial banks and MFIs to collect and report on gender-disaggregated data. IGREENFIN I proposes an action plan for all banks which help them integrate or strengthen their M&E systems to ensure that such gender-disaggregated data is collected. Of the loans granted by the Green Financing Facility, 50 per cent are to go to women-led and youth-led MSMEs, FOs and cooperatives. Under Output 2.3, IGREENFIN I will incentive and promote the participation of women-led MSMEs and FOs in capacity-building and technical assistance activity. Furthermore, IGREENFIN I will support awareness-raising campaigns and set clear gender-sensitive targeting strategies. IGREENFIN I will tackle many barriers to women's access to financial services and credit, including gendered division of labour, property rights and control over assets; lack or no awareness of financing opportunities and new instruments like green financing; lack of access to knowledge, particularly reliable climate information, and biased perception of the financial sector. Key activities to be financed by the green sub loans are concrete measures to reduce women's workloads, agri-preneurship support for women's organizations, etc. Working with men is critical, as they are often the gatekeepers of customary practices that limit women's access

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.1 | PAGE 114 OF 116

## BUDGETING

281. The countries involved in IGREENFIN I, represented by their NEEs, will be recipients of the IGREENFIN I loan and grant proceeds and guarantee the repayment of the GCF loan portion. The NEEs will sign the relevant on lending agreements with each of the LNABs to implement the loan and grant proceeds.

IFAD RCU:
282. Each year, the IFAD RCU will communicate the budget timetable and templates to be used for submission to the Baseline PMUs, which must comply with GCF guidelines. The IFAD RCU will emphasize that the IGREENFIN I overhead costs should be disclosed as a separate component of budget and will be funded through the IGREENFIN I grant proceeds.

283. Upon receipt of the country's IGREENFIN I budget from each Baseline PMU, the IFAD RCU will review and ensure that each budget line item includes a note / description of underlying assumptions, harmonize the budgets and coordinate the obtention of IFAD non objection letter for each country's IGREE NFIN I budget. As soon as the non objection letters are obtained from IFAD, the IFAD RCU will consolidate them into a single IGREENFIN I budget, ensuring that the IFAD RCU's grant budget is incorporated prior to submission to the GCF.

## Baseline PMU:

284. The Baseline PMU staff will closely collaborate with the bank PIUs throughout the AWPB preparation process, from inception to reception of IFAD's non objection letter. This is to ensure that IFAD/GCF budget guidelines are followed. Once the final draft budget of the bank PIU is submitted to the Baseline PMU, the Baseline PMU will check that it is multi dimensional and complies with both budget guidelines and that the appropriate budget templates have been used in the budget submitted. Once the Baseline PMU is satisfied with the content and presentation of the Bank's IGREENFIN I budget, it will incorporate its portion of the grant budget into the budget and submit the draft budget to the RCU for comments and review before convening the NSC for review and approval, following its normal AWPB timetable. Once the NSC approves the budget, it is officially submitted to IFAD for non objection.

## BANK PIU:

285. The bank PIU will prepare the IGREENFIN I budget while paying attention to the milestones in the timetable communicated by the Baseline PMU. Most importantly, they must submit the final draft to the baseline PMU no later than 1 October every year and use prescribe templates. The budget will be broken down by donor, component / subcomponent / activity, category / sub-category of expenditure, zone / subzone, etc. The loan component of the budget will be drawn up from the forecast activities of target beneficiaries and RFIs while the grant component is drawn from the indirect costs required to operate the scheme.

## ACCOUNTING STANDARDS AND FINANCIAL REPORTING:

286. Each EE will use its own accounting standards though its external auditors. ToRs must clearly state that they will prepare IGREENFIN I financial statements in accordance with IFRS. Refer to Section 6 of annex 22 for list of Financial Reports that the IFAD RCU will submit to the GCF, each Baseline PMU to the IFAD RCU, and each Bank PIU to their respective Baseline PMU. The role of the IFAD RCU Financial Unit is to harmonize, compile and consolidate country financial reports for submission to GCF. The baseline project will review and ensure that the format and content of Bank PIU financial reports comply with reporting guidelines prior to submission to IFAD RCU. Bank PIUs will ensure that they can generate the mandated financial reports in the format required by the Baseline PMU using their Financial Reporting System. All financial information related to IGREENFIN I must be expressed in the currency stipulated in the IFAD Financing Agreement, namely US dollars.

287. Procurement: Procurement for the programme will be carried out in accordance with IFAD applicable rules and practices, as well as the IFAD's Programme Implementation Manual (PIM), the programme specific PIM and loan administration arrangements established through the Letter to the Borrower and the Financing Agreement between IFAD and each National Borrower/Recipient.
