To: The Green Climate Fund (“GCF”)  

Abidjan, 17 May 2021  

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by The International Fund For Agricultural Development (IFAD) regarding “Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase I”  

Dear Yannick GLemareck,  
Executive Director of the Green Climate Fund,  

We refer to “Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase I” in Côte d’Ivoire as included in the funding proposal submitted by IFAD to us on 29 April 2020.  

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the National Designated Authority/Focal point of the republic of Côte d’Ivoire.  

Pursuant to GCF decision B.08/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase I as included in the funding proposal.  

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:  
(a) The government of The Republic of Senegal has no-objection to “Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in 13 countries of the Green Great Wall (GGW). Phase I” as included in the funding proposal;  
(b) The “Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase I” as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with The Côte d’Ivoire’s national priorities, strategies and plans;  
(c) In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, The “Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase I” as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards.

Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder  
Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase I" in Djibouti as included in the

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the National Designated  
Authority/Focal point of Djibouti.

Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission  
Smallholder Agriculture in 13 countries of the Green Great

# [Page 1]

The "Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase I" as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with Djibouti's national priorities, strategies and plans;

(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguard, The "Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase I" as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations,

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the "Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase I" as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We also confirm that our no-objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Kind regards,

Mr. Dini Abdallah Omar  
Secretary General, Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development  
Djibouti  

GCF-Focal Point-Djibouti

# UNITE-TRAVAIL-PROGRES REPUBLIDUE DU TCHAD

## N'Djamena, le 23 juin 2021

### MINISTERE DE LENVIRONNEMENT, DE LA PECHE ET DU DEVELOPPEMENT DURABLE

#### w0tMEPDo/PNEFVC/ Z021

---

**The FocalPoint**

To Mr. Yannick Glemarec  
Director Executive of Green Climate Fund, G-Tower, 24-4 Songado dang, Yeonsu-gu, Incheon, City Republic of Korea  

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by The International Fund For Agricultural Development (IFAD) regarding "Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase II"

Dear Yannick Glemarec,

We refer to "Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase II" in Chadas included in the funding proposal submitted by IFAD to us on 10 June 2021.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the National Designated Authority/Focal point of the Republic of Chad.

Pursuant to GCF decision B.08/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase II as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of The Republic of Chad has no objection to "Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase II" as included in the funding proposal;

(b) The "Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase II" as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with The Chad's national priorities, strategies and plans;

(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, The "Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase II" as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the "Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN): Greening Agricultural Banks & Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Green Great Wall (GGW) countries. Phase II" as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We also confirm that our no-objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Kind regards,  
Mr. IBrahim Charfadine Abdelkerim  
FP/ National Designated Authority of Chad for the Green Climate fund

# GCF/B.31/02/Add.02  
Page 149  

## Secretariat’s assessment of FP183  

### Proposal name: Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN I): Greening Agricultural Banks & the Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Great Green Wall (GGW) Countries – Phase I  

- **Accredited entity:** International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)  
- **Country/(ies):** IGREENFIN I [Components 1 and 2]: Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal  
- **GCF GGW Regional Support Programme [Component 3]:** Burkina Faso, Chad, Djibouti, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, Senegal and Sudan  
- **Project/programme size:** Medium  

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat  

### 1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:  

#### Strengths  
- The programme will enhance access to credit and technical assistance for local farmers, farmers’ organizations, cooperatives and micro and small sized enterprises to help them implement climate-resilient and low-emission agriculture/agroforestry and value chain processing and mini-grid supply linked to adaptive supply chains.  

#### Points of caution  
- The use of GCF funds exclusively for climate-adaptive and mitigation practices need to be ensured.  
- The programme will incentivize the participation of the private sector by engaging with commercial banks and microfinance institutions.  
- The programme is subject to project-specific risks, such as macroeconomic, political, project implementation, and environmental and social risks.  
- The programme provides a framework for the current and future GCF development portfolio in the Sahel to share knowledge; scale up successful interventions in mitigation and adaptation; provide a platform for results monitoring and evaluation; and build a platform of digital and technical innovations to support farmer groups.  

### 2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum V, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations”, respectively.  

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment  

### 2.1 Programme background

# GCF/B.31/02/Add.02  
## Page 150  

### 3. The 11 countries of the Great Green Wall (GGW), as well as Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana, are among the world's poorest and most vulnerable countries to climate change. They ranked at the bottom of the Human Development Index in 2020 and a large majority of their population lacks access to employment, basic health care, education and natural resources, which contributes to food insecurity, youth migration and conflict. Agriculture, livestock and forestry activities are the foundations of their economies and more than 70 per cent of rural communities depend directly on rainfed agriculture (crops, livestock, fisheries and forestry) for nutrition and livelihoods. The agriculture sector is extremely vulnerable to both climate change and variability owing to the geographical location of these countries and their socioeconomic and technological characteristics. Over recent decades, climate change has increased the frequency, intensity and duration of droughts and floods, temperature variability, desertification, water stress and soil erosion, all of which reduce agricultural productivity and food security. Shocks from climate change and adverse weather conditions – resulting in the loss of assets, crops and livestock, disruptions in value chains and soaring food prices – in addition to the effects of the coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) pandemic are pushing millions of smallholder farmers further into poverty.

### 4. Climate projections indicate that precipitation levels will continue to decrease, while temperatures are expected to increase by between 1 and 1.72 °C during the period 2031–2050 compared with the 1986–2005 reference period. According to these projections, agricultural production is expected to decrease by at least 20 per cent in the region, which will reduce food availability and farmers' incomes, drive competition over diminishing natural capital and lead to greater instability and migration, especially among young people in rural areas. Climate change also destabilizes local markets and curbs economic growth and investment, which in turn affects job creation, particularly for young people and women. It will therefore make it more difficult to overcome the current lack of access to credit and technical capacity barriers, one of the primary barriers limiting the growth of rural smallholder farmers’ organizations, cooperatives and micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) in the GGW region.

### 5. Increasing access to financing is key to improving on-farm productivity by providing working capital, allowing for investment in post-harvest value aggregation of products and

# GCF/B.31/02/Add.02  
## Page 151  

The proposed IGREENFIN I programme has three components:  

### Component 1: Green Business Financing Facility  
This will be established to provide concessional loans to foster best adaptation and mitigation practices for green businesses within the selected agricultural value chains in each target country. Operated by local national agricultural banks (LNABs), the facility will offer special lines of financing for green business projects developed by farmers’ organizations, women and youth organizations, cooperatives and MSMEs (including agribusiness actors and operators of solar photovoltaic energy systems).  

### Component 2: Technical Assistance Facility (TAF)  
This will be created to address capacity, knowledge and policy gaps hindering the uptake of green business projects in the selected countries. It will target LNABs, central banks and clients (farmers’ organizations, MSMEs and cooperatives), with a special emphasis on women and youth.  

### Component 3: GGW Regional Support Programme  
To address the above-mentioned increasingly complex and interrelated challenges and support the transition towards climate-resilient, low-emission agriculture, the GGW initiative was launched in 2007 by the African Union. The initial objectives of the GGW initiative were to address land degradation, climate change adaptation and mitigation, and protect biodiversity and forests.  

There is a need to accelerate investment in the region to address these issues, especially since the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic, which has had severe impacts on the region’s economic development. In a post-COVID-19 context, the ambition of the GGW initiative is to pursue a green growth recovery by restoring 100 million hectares of currently degraded land, sequestering 250 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MtCO2eq) and creating 10 million green jobs in rural areas by 2030.  

### Output 3.1 – Enhanced knowledge management and exchange  
The GGW Regional Support Programme component will increase the collective impacts both of individual GCF projects, as well as those undertaken in close collaboration with the Global Environment Facility (GEF), and programmes (including IGREENFIN I) through two outputs:  

(i) Output 3.1 – enhanced knowledge management and exchange to accelerate the uptake of good practices, increase learning and inform policy and investments across GCF and other projects;  

(ii) Output 3.2 – mapping innovative and digital transformation technologies and building a digital and innovative ecosystem.  

### IGREENFIN I Funding  
IGREENFIN I seeks EUR 73,971,000 in loans and EUR 30,444,189 in technical assistance grants from GCF. Additional co-financing is being mobilized from IFAD (USD 40 million), the Islamic Development Bank (USD 15 million) and the African Development Bank (USD 18 million).  

The GCF resources will be innovatively structured to stimulate and create a market for green business and will provide additional climate finance to complement IFAD baseline investments.  

The programme will support:  

(i) the acceleration of the transfer of climate-resilient, low-carbon technologies;  

(ii) the development of innovative green business models for

# GCF/B.31/02/Add.02  
## Page 152  

### Agricultural value chain projects, pipeline planning and deployment; and (iii) the alignment of the portfolios and pipelines of the targeted LNABs with each country’s nationally determined contribution (NDC) through capacity-building, policy dialogue and knowledge-building activities. This funding proposal covers only IGREENFIN I Phase I and the GGW Regional Support Programme.

### 9. IGREENFIN I intends to directly build the resilience and adaptive capacity of 378,600 smallholder farmers organized around 1,500 MSMEs and 2,500 farmers’ organizations or cooperatives. The number of indirect beneficiaries of the programme is estimated at 2,494,000 in the five selected countries (Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal), of which 50 per cent will be women and 50 per cent will be men (comprising 50 per cent youth and 50 per cent adult males), by increasing their climate resilience through access to affordable long-term green loans. The programme is expected to reduce 225,487 tCO2eq annually (5,637,175 tCO2eq over a 25-year lifespan of investments) from agriculture, forestry and land use, energy use and power generation.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria  
#### 3.1 Impact potential                 Scale: Medium to high  

### 10. IGREENFIN I will support the greening of the financial system in the five selected countries (Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal) by building the capacity of five LNABs to offer green financing for climate-resilient, low-emission agriculture projects (green governance and lending products, green credit indicator systems, long-term loan periods and unlocking of barriers to innovative climate finance). It will also support 1,500 MSMEs and 2,500 farmers’ organizations and cooperatives, as well as the transfer or licensing of 200 technologies and innovative solutions for climate-resilient, low-emission agricultural development. Its interventions will contribute to reducing the targeted countries’ greenhouse gas emissions by 5,637,184 tCO2eq over the 25-year lifetime of the programme. The adaptation benefits of this programme are estimated to reach 378,600 direct and 2,494,000 indirect beneficiaries, of which 50 per cent will be women and 50 per cent will be men (comprising 50 per cent youth and 50 per cent adult males). Innovative green business models, including climate-resistant breeds and crop varieties, sustainable land restoration techniques, solar energy to power drip irrigation, and processing, packaging and storage of equipment, for the 10 selected value chains (cattle, chicken, tomato, sustainable mango agro-forestry, cashew, shea, rice, groundnut, millet, cassava production), provide an indication of how the EUR 177,481,678 in requested funding for IGREENFIN I could be used to produce positive impacts and ensure both financial and economic returns on investment. The green business models could be scaled up by the financial sector in each of the targeted countries and in the GGW region.

### 11. IGREENFIN I will contribute to the implementation of the GGW initiative, which aims to restore 100 million hectares of currently degraded land, sequester 250 MtCO2eq and create 10 million jobs in rural areas by 2030. Spanning the entire width of Africa – 8,000 km from Senegal to Djibouti – the “Wall” is a mosaic of projects implemented by countries in the region with the support of development partners as a pan-African sustainable landscape programme to address land degradation and desertification, boost food, environmental and economic security, and support the adaptation of communities to climate change. With the aim of achieving much more than just growing trees, its goals are to (i) foster climate resilience among local communities in a region expected to experience warming temperatures; (ii) safeguard food security for the 20 million people in the Sahel that lack sufficient food every year; (iii) support the creation of green jobs to provide stable incomes for African women and youth; (iv) build a global response to the migration crisis, with 60 million people set to migrate to Europe from degraded parts of Africa by 2021; and (v) foster peace and security in the Sahel, where conflict has already displaced 2.8 million people.

# GCF/B.31/02/Add.02  
## Page 153  

The proposed climate-resilient agriculture interventions, such as crop rotation, intercropping, agroforestry and zai-planting techniques, are well-tested solutions in Africa and are aligned with good practices for achieving adaptation results. If implemented robustly and sustained after programme completion, such interventions can lead to transformational changes in the behaviours of farmers, as well as positive impacts on the livelihoods of the target beneficiaries. It is recommended that climate-resilient and low-emission technologies and solutions to transform agricultural practices in the medium and long term are explored and used.

IGREENFIN aims to engage local national agricultural banks, national banking associations, banking regulators, green standard-setting bodies, local universities and business schools in green finance in the agriculture sector. Through the programme’s mainstreaming work, these institutions will be able to sustain the GCF grant funded activities and concessionality beyond the programme’s duration. The programme will support the development of studies and policy papers on climate risk management to define the relevant standards, and develop and harmonize green lending products, instruments and tools such as environmental and social governance benchmarks. It will also build knowledge on green finance invested in the agriculture sector by both the private sector and governments.

IGREENFIN will work closely with national agricultural banks to develop and improve their environmental and social governance and internal capacity, and better align part of their lending portfolio on NDCs. By building the banks’ capacity to offer affordable green finance for smallholders in the agriculture sector, the programme will start to fill the gap in financing for agriculture and alter the perception of smallholders from a risky business to a vast market with great potential and opportunities.

Green credit lines and awareness campaigns will support the development of demand for green products, particularly among youth and women, support a viable pipeline of green business projects, reduce the financial sector’s perception that smallholders present a high risk and hence promote the development of new green markets in sectors that have been traditionally difficult to reach (e.g. the MSME sector). Beneficiaries such as MSMEs will benefit from training activities and affordable finance (available from local financial institutions) and techniques that help them reduce their operating costs while increasing their productivity and income.

The early stages of the programme’s operations are expected to demonstrate to end users the effectiveness and value of the solutions proposed, which will stimulate demand beyond the target financial institutions and countries to other market sectors and areas covered by GGW after the programme ends. This programme will enable the transition away from grant-funded rural finance projects towards sustainable long-term markets for such projects.

The GGW Regional Support Programme introduces a new approach piloted by IFAD and GCF, which aims to catalyse impact beyond a one-off project or programme investment by creating bridges for knowledge-sharing and learning between GCF projects that would otherwise be implemented in silos, and among accredited entities (AEs), GCF national designated authorities (NDAs), and GGW and GEF focal points. This innovative approach has the potential to be scaled up in Africa beyond the GGW countries and could also be replicated in other large landscapes and ecosystems such as the Amazon or in countries with relatively

# GCF/B.31/02/Add.02  
## Page 154  

The proposal demonstrates good potential for scaling up and replication at the country and regional levels. The grant component is essential as it will lead to tangible knowledge management for scaling up and replication of sustainable lending.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential           Scale: High  

The proposal demonstrates high potential for sustainable development. IGREENFIN I and the GGW Regional Support Programme have identified and adopted the objectives of the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and mapped each of the target sectors to them: No poverty (SDG 1); Zero hunger (SDG 2); Gender equality (SDG 5); Clean water and sanitation (SDG 6); Affordable and clean energy (SDG 7); Decent work and economic growth (SDG 8); Climate action (SDG 13); Life on land (SDG 15); and Partnerships for the Goals (SDG 17).

The programme is designed to help secure multiple environmental benefits for water, air, land and nature by promoting the adoption of best adaptation and mitigation practices and technology, which will enhance the sustainable management of natural resources, protect threatened dryland biodiversity against erosion and desertification, and increase the potential for carbon sequestration. These benefits could also contribute to the increased resilience of the region’s ecosystems and human livelihoods to climate change and variability and the overall objectives of GGW (land restoration, job creation and carbon dioxide (CO₂) emission reduction).

The programme intends to reduce the use of energy from biomass, including firewood and agricultural residues, imported diesel fuel and charcoal, which are the main sources of electricity in the five selected countries, by providing greater access to renewable energy. It is expected that CO₂ emissions will be reduced (total emission reductions of 5.6 MtCO₂eq) during the programme’s lifecycle through improved agroforestry and sustainable land management practices (this emission reduction will further increase once the avoided emissions from renewable energy technologies (RET) are included) and limits will be placed on the use of biomass and diesel for energy consumption through the adoption of RET.

With the promotion of EbA and agroforestry techniques, more than 100,000 hectares of degraded land will be restored and sustainably managed with improved ecosystem goods and services. The programme will build up each link in the selected agricultural value chains (including livestock and non-timber forest products) from production to markets, increasing the productivity of key commodities and farmers’ incomes while safeguarding the environment.

The activities proposed under this programme will generate multiple job opportunities for people in rural areas, particularly for youth and women, promote inclusion and support coherent investment through farmers’ organizations, cooperatives and MSMEs. By promoting local low-emission and climate-resilient options, the programme will contribute to cost-saving health benefits due to cleaner air for local communities and access to clean energy, water and food, thus reducing food insecurity. As described in its gender analysis and action plan, the programme will support the participation of women in decision-making, facilitate their access to financing and assets and improve their livelihoods. The targeted areas considered the most vulnerable and selected through IFAD baseline investments will be positively impacted by the programme.

The economic benefits of this programme are linked to its high potential to reduce poverty through increased productivity of key crops and livestock production, as well as income-generation activities, such as the production of and value added to a variety of fruit and

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient                                                  Scale: High  

23. The vulnerability of the country and target populations and their adaptation needs are  
explained in the funding proposal and feasibility study, including a general analysis of the scale  
and intensity of exposure to climate change risks. The countries participating in this programme  
are highly vulnerable to drought, flooding, rising temperatures and variability of rainfall, as well  
as other climate change hazards (e.g. wildfires and locust infestations). Their exposure and  
vulnerability to climate hazards is projected to increase in the future. Climate projections  
suggest that the Western Sahel and northern parts of Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana will be hotter and  
drier with more frequent extreme events. Under most scenarios, temperature rise will  
accelerate, meaning that the continent of Africa could be between 2 and 6 °C warmer by the end  
of this century (African Center for Meteorological Applications for Development, 2020). Climate  
impacts have negative impacts on agriculture. They reduce productivity, food security and gross  
domestic product, cause endemic diseases (e.g. malaria) and increase rural poverty. Given the  
five countries’ high poverty rate (see the feasibility study for further details) and the impacts of  
climate change, the countries have few financial institutions willing to risk investing in  
smallholder farming, despite the fact that agriculture remains the main source of economic  
growth.  

24. The five countries’ financial systems remain weak and have a limited number of  
instruments for financing agriculture. LNABs have limited possibilities of mobilizing highly  
concessional loans in capital markets. Farmers’ organizations, MSMEs, cooperatives, and youth  
and women-led organizations active in the agriculture sector often lack access to long-term  
financing with affordable conditions (interest rates, tenors and grace periods) due to small loan  
sizes and relatively high transaction costs. Key barriers are limited adaptation capacity to  
 climate change at the country level, lack of skills and knowledge of climate finance and climate-  
resilient, low-emission agriculture, the nascent regulatory frameworks and the need to de-risk  
the agriculture and banking sectors.  

25. Technical assistance and funding for the establishment of a Green Business Financing  
Facility that will provide concessional loans and climate-smart credit risk tools will enhance  
access to credit, contribute to de-risking the sector and foster greater knowledge of climate  
finance and adaptation across the selected countries. This will directly help LNABs to overcome  
the key barriers they face in relation to providing access to finance to rural populations, and  
enable them to engage in green financing.

## 3.5 Country ownership               Scale: High  

26. The proposal notes the programme’s alignment with the participating countries’ national climate change policies and strategies and NDCs. It is recommended that the programme’s progress and results are monitored and quantified, and the extent to which the programme contributes to the countries’ NDCs is measured.  

27. The participating countries are Parties to the UNFCCC and have signed and ratified the Kyoto Protocol. By ratifying the UNFCCC, these countries have committed to implement measures to adapt to climate change and report on their NDCs. This programme will contribute to the implementation of the objectives of the three Rio Conventions ratified by all countries¹⁶, including the UNFCCC, as well as the Paris Agreement, the SDGs and the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015–2030.  

28. This programme is fully aligned with the participating countries’ national development plans and national commitments on climate mitigation and adaptation included in their national adaptation programmes of action, national climate change policies and strategies, NDCs, national communications and national strategies for disaster risk reduction, as well as the SDGs.  

## 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness               Scale: Medium to high  

29. In the GGW region, green businesses are largely financially underserved, both in terms of availability of highly concessional loans from LNABs, commercial banks and microfinance institutions and in terms of technical assistance. Activities under IGREENFIN I aim to fill a financing gap through the provision of financial and non-financial support. In doing so, the programme will boost a nascent market that is currently far from tapping into its full potential. As this market develops over time, opportunities will be created to attract other public and private sector investors in these least developed countries (Burkina Faso, Mali and Senegal) and lower-middle income countries (Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana) that share the features of the least developed countries, namely a high level of poverty, given the size of the population and extreme vulnerability to climate change.  

30. IGREENFIN I will promote and scale up green finance products and green business models in the targeted countries and region. By building synergies and leveraging IGREENFIN I resources, the programme will reach the maximum number of beneficiaries. Partnerships with LNABs and executing entities (EEs) will be promoted through the provision of green loans and technical assistance to de-risk all potential subprojects.  

31. GCF financing will overcome the existing barriers to accessing financial and non-financial services that hinder the adoption and implementation of best climate change adaptation and mitigation measures (e.g. solar energy for agriculture). Additionally, even though LNABs have been performing quite well over recent years with various loan products, their loans are still not sufficiently concessional to stimulate greater adherence to low-emission and climate-resilient agriculture and generate a strong pipeline of green business projects. In the energy sector, several bilateral donors have put in place concessional financing schemes that combine grants with credit to promote agricultural and rural finance; they work mainly through microfinance institutions and State-linked financial institutions, such as the targeted LNABs. Early results from these programmes are highly encouraging, although strong oversight and technical assistance are necessary. IGREENFIN I is projected to achieve a leverage ratio of 1:0.7 (i.e. USD 0.7 of additional public and private funding leveraged per USD 1 of GCF investment); thus, the GCF investment is expected to leverage about USD 90 million in additional public and private contributions (including co-finance).  

¹⁶ The Rio Conventions consist of the Convention on Biological Diversity, the United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification and the UNFCCC.

## IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies  

### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards  

### 34. The programme aims to support the building and scaling up of the resilience and adaptive capacity of farmers’ organizations, cooperatives and MSMEs by increasing the beneficiaries’ access to concessional credit lines for green agricultural investments that would result in low greenhouse gas emissions and climate resilient and sustainable agricultural practices in the target countries. The environmental co-benefits envisaged include enhancing the sustainability of natural resources, protecting threatened dryland biodiversity, and preventing erosion and desertification through the adoption of adaptation and mitigation practices and technologies. The social co-benefits for local communities include improved health due to cleaner air and increased access to clean energy, water and food, and enhanced food security.  

### 35. Environmental and social risk category and safeguard instrument. The programme is categorized as medium level of intermediation (I-2), because the bulk of the GCF support is allocated to providing concessional loans to beneficiaries, through the LNABs, for adaptation and mitigation practices and businesses. The programme will not finance category A or high-risk subprojects, nor any investments listed in the programme exclusion list (e.g. activities that lead to or require involuntary resettlement or involve conversion or degradation of critical natural habitat, among others). An environmental and social management framework (ESMF) that outlines the potential risks and impact of the proposed programme and the mitigation measures to address these impacts has been prepared.  

### 36. Compliance with GCF’s environmental and social safeguards standards. Paragraphs below describe how the programme proposes to address the requirements of the GCF environmental and social safeguard (ESS) standards.  

### 37. ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts. The AE has prepared an ESMF that provides a description of how environmental and social impact and risk will be managed, mitigated and supervised at both the framework level and the programme operational level. The ESMF identifies the potential direct and indirect risks as well as both positive and negative risks and impacts of the activities, and provides plans and strategies on how the adverse risks and impacts will be avoided, minimized and/or mitigated.

# GCF/B.31/02/Add.02  
## Page 158  

### 38. ESS 2: Labour and working conditions  
Given the generally poor working conditions and limited awareness of and non-compliance with health and safety standards in the agriculture and energy sectors in the rural areas in the target countries, risks related to labour and working conditions (e.g., child or forced labour) in financed subprojects may occur. Such risks may occur in programme activities that may require the participation of external service providers, such as during the installation of renewable energy technologies, the installation and maintenance of irrigation systems, or the drilling of boreholes, where underage workers may be engaged.  

The strategies that the programme intends to implement include screening and monitoring the service providers to ensure that any forms of child and forced labour in infrastructure construction related to the programme will not occur. The programme also includes training and capacity-building on existing labour regulations, as well as implementing a forced and child labour surveillance mechanism as part of the regular supervision visits of the programme during implementation. These actions are part of the AE’s regular monitoring across its implementing partners.  

While most of the beneficiaries will be small-scale farmers who may invest in improving their agricultural practices, some beneficiaries (particularly the MSMEs) may employ workers, in which case the programme intends to ensure that they respect both the national standards and standards set by the International Labour Organization.  

### 39. ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention  
The programme’s activities include supporting “carbon sensitive processing and packaging”, which could result in factories causing noise and air pollution, solid waste generation as a result of packaging waste, and water and soil pollution from factory effluents.  

To mitigate these risks, the programme requires participants to install pollution control technologies such as scrubbers, locate new factories in industrial zones or far from communities, promote the use of recyclable or reusable packaging materials, and develop waste and water management plans.  

To promote efficient use of resources, the programme will promote strategies such as using renewable energy sources as well as retrofitting with modern and efficient equipment.  

Regarding the potential release of agrochemicals such as fertilizers into water bodies during agricultural production, the programme will provide training to farmers on proper use of fertilizers and agrochemicals; and a pesticide management framework and plan is included in the ESMF.  

Considering there is environmental and health risk from potentially hazardous wastes in the RET installations (e.g., solar panels and batteries containing contaminants such as lithium and lead), the programme will develop a hazardous waste management framework/plan covering the operational and decommissioning phases of the installations to ensure that feasible actions for the proper management of the wastes are established.  

To ensure that borehole drilling and irrigation expansion activities will not result in overextraction, these activities will be required to comply with nationally and/or internationally established guidance, and the programme will require a supply/demand assessment to be conducted prior to implementation to ensure that there is adequate supply for all users in the targeted area.  

### 40. ESS 4: Community health, safety and security  
The programme may have environmental health and safety related issues or impacts that may affect the communities as a result of the activities of the programme. These include health risks related to noise and air pollution, inadequate handling of RET wastes, release of agrochemicals into the surroundings and exposure to pesticides.  

The management measures planned to be implemented include training and capacity-building activities and development of hazardous waste and pesticide management plans.  

Potential risk of social conflicts, sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH), and potential use of forced and/or child labour in the implementation of the programme’s activities in the target countries will be mitigated through implementation of a “no tolerance” policy for SEAH in the programme’s supported operations to ensure that appropriate precautionary and remedial measures for any occurrences of SEAH risks and/or complaints are incorporated in the programme design and are embodied in the AE’s policy on SEAH.  

Regarding the potential for conflicts relating to the agriculture-based activities, the programme will ensure that prospective beneficiaries are made well aware of selection criteria in the selected project districts and regions.

# GCF/B.31/02/Add.02  
## Page 159  

### 41. ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement  
The programme’s activities will likely involve involuntary resettlement and land acquisition, particularly for activities that may require population resettlement (e.g. for agricultural production sites or agricultural produce processing and packaging facilities). These activities are not yet clearly defined so a resettlement policy framework, which will include provisions for the development and implementation of site-specific resettlement action plans, will be developed during programme implementation, taking into consideration the legal requirements in each of the countries. The action plans will then be developed as needed, if the activities under the programme lead to land acquisition and involuntary resettlement.

### 42. ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources  
The activities under the programme will have a potential risk of habitat fragmentation through the development of production plots or infrastructure construction, as well as the potential risk of the introduction of alien or invasive species. To mitigate these impacts, the ESMF stipulates that critical biodiversity hotspots in target areas will be identified and mapped at the start of the programme for inclusion in the eligibility criteria. The mapping activity will be conducted by the programme’s safeguards team in collaboration with the environmental protection agencies in the target countries so that the alien or invasive species identified can be included in the final project eligibility criteria.  

The programme as a whole will not support activities that will lead to conversion of wetlands, conversion of significant areas of natural forests or other wild lands into agricultural or pastureland; nor will it support activities that will lead to loss of natural habitats, biodiversity or decreased provision of ecosystem services in sensitive areas.  

The programme will provide capacity-building activities aimed at strengthening the capabilities of local and indigenous populations on natural resource management and safeguard issues.

### 43. GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous peoples  
Pastoralists and other socially vulnerable groups are present in the countries where the program activities will be undertaken, and therefore the requirements of the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy will need to be considered and applied by the AE.  

The programme excludes activities that could lead to adverse social impacts to local communities, or that could result in displacement of at least 20 people. Within its ESMF framework, the AE provides the following entry points to ensure the consideration of indigenous peoples: an indigenous peoples plan outline that provides the framework for planning to exclude adverse impacts on indigenous peoples; and a rapid social analysis template that provides the basis to understand the baseline and social diversity at the subproject sites.  

The stakeholder engagement plan includes a distinct strategy for engaging with indigenous peoples.  

The AE notes that its free, prior and informed consent requirements apply not only to indigenous peoples and historically underserved local communities but also to other local rural communities whose land access and use rights may be affected.  

In terms of potential benefits for indigenous peoples, some targeted opportunities for indigenous peoples and local communities can be found in components 1, 2 and 3, and indicators for the number of beneficiary organizations composed principally of members of ethnic minority backgrounds as well as the engagement of vulnerable groups could be used to promote the participation of indigenous peoples.  

The programme will promote the benefits of indigenous knowledge by including the strengthening of indigenous climate risk management capabilities in its screening form that is used for evaluating the climate change vulnerability of applicants.  

A budget for training on indigenous peoples screening and development of an indigenous peoples plan is included under the budget for ESMF monitoring.

### 44. ESS 8: Cultural heritage  
The programme will exclude activities that will lead to conversion and/or loss of physical cultural resources, and this will be part of its screening criteria. Should instances where chance finds (of physical cultural resources that are not identified during screening) are discovered during construction and/or operation of the facilities, the programme will redirect the work into other sites to prevent any damage.  

Adequate “chance find” procedures in the operational guidelines of external service providers.

### 45. Implementation arrangements  
The AE will ensure coordination, implementation and monitoring of the environmental and social performance of the programme, and third-party sector-specific consultants may be brought on board to support the implementation of the environmental and social management plans. A regional coordination unit will be established and will have responsibility for the overall operational implementation of the ESMF. An ESS specialist will be engaged within the RCU to oversee the planning, delivery and monitoring of all safeguards-related activities. At the national level, a project management unit will be established within each bank, and the PMU will oversee the supervision and development of the subprojects, and ensure effective implementation of the ESMF guidelines among its contractors and sub-contractors. The banks will also be responsible for the environmental and social assessment, and management and monitoring of the subprojects under their respective portfolios. The AE’s RCU will evaluate and monitor the supported banks on a continuous basis throughout the project cycle, including coordinating with the finance ministries in the target countries to monitor their environmental and social performance.  

### 46. Stakeholder engagement and grievance redress mechanism  
The programme has conducted various stakeholder consultations in each of the target countries during the baseline investment design stage. A stakeholder engagement strategy and engagement plan has also been developed to define programme context, principles of stakeholder engagement, applicable national and international regulations, analysis of programme stakeholders, approaches to stakeholder engagement, communication and information disclosure, grievance mechanism, and monitoring and reporting of stakeholder engagement activities. The programme will also require its borrowers to implement effective grievance mechanisms to receive and assist with the resolution of any concerns and grievances that stakeholders may raise in connection with the programme’s environmental and social performance. This is in addition to the AE’s institutional complaints procedure for receiving and resolving concerns and issues relating to non-compliance with its environmental and social policies and procedures  

## 4.2 Gender policy  

### 47. The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of the GCF.  

### 48. The gender assessment provides the context of the enabling environment in the five countries that are the focus of the program. It indicates that all five countries have ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), as well as a number of other international conventions including the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (Maputo Protocol). The constitutions of the respective countries also reflect an ambition for equality between women and men, and prohibition of discrimination based on gender. Each of the countries have established women’s ministries/agencies with responsibilities of implementing, promoting and protecting the rights of women and other marginalized community members. Despite supportive institutional arrangements, legal frameworks and policies, IGREENFIN-targeted countries remain among the countries with the lowest rankings when it comes to gender equality and equity indicators. While Senegal ranks higher than the other focal countries, gender gaps remain high across all countries due to social and cultural norms; a lack of enforcement and a lack of implementation of laws and policies; and a lack of resources dedicated to reducing gender gaps.   

### 49. The gender assessment was completed based on a desk review. The gender assessment found that gender inequalities persist in the agriculture and finance sectors of the five countries targeted by the IGREENFIN regional programme. This is particularly relevant when it comes to women’s access to credit for agriculture, as well as gender inequalities for accessing other

# GCF/B.31/02/Add.02  
## Page 161  

The gendered division of labour in households places the burden of securing water and fuel supplies, and caring for the children and the elderly largely on women, leaving them with very little time to engage in income-generating activities or to further their education. Women in the region have less access to skilled jobs, and are marginalized in the labour market in and outside the agricultural sector. They represent only 8 per cent of entrepreneurs.

Gender-based inequalities exist in terms of participation in formal labour markets; and gender-based inequalities in agriculture also apply to access to and control over resources, which affect women’s agricultural productivity. These gender inequalities exist in land ownership, access to inputs, access to financial resources, and access to information and training, among other things. Existing financial service providers and their products also generally fail to consider the particular needs of women and rarely conduct gender-based assessments when developing products.

Cultural and societal norms continue to create barriers for women’s engagement in leadership and decision-making. In addition, gender-based violence is prevalent throughout the five focal countries.

50. The AE has provided a gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirement of the Gender Policy of the GCF. The gender action plan provides activities that address the challenges faced by women and includes baseline, indicators, targets, timelines and budget.

51. The gender action plan includes activities designed to increase access to finance for climate-resilient agricultural practices and enhanced utilization of RETs by women-led households, married women in households, youth, minority groups, farmers’ organizations, cooperatives and MSMEs and increase women’s participation in governance and decision-making in the energy finance sector. More specifically, the gender action plan includes activities to increase access to credit for farmers’ organizations, enterprises and cooperatives managed or owned by women and young people. This includes activities designed to improve women’s property rights and control over assets, and capacity-building for farmers’ organizations, enterprises and cooperatives managed or owned by women and youth.

The AE will build the capacity of banks and national ministries in gender mainstreaming and the development of institutional frameworks that facilitate women’s engagement in decision-making processes. The action plan also includes the development of financial products that take into account the key barriers that women and women-run organizations traditionally face. Communication campaigns via visits, social media and radio, directed at raising awareness and understanding of renewable energy products and financial products will be undertaken, with women and women-run organizations as key target audiences. Award programmes that encourage the participation of women and youth run organizations implementing renewable energy projects are also part of the plan.

The AE will hire a full-time gender specialist within each bank participating in the programme and ensure that gender is integrated throughout the program. This includes ensuring gender expertise in decision-making bodies. It will also develop a conflict resolution and grievance mechanism that will provide women with easy access to these mechanisms and the ability to report and address instances of gender-based violence.

52. As the gender assessment was based on a desk review, there are further studies to be conducted in order to ensure the engagement of stakeholders from different groups. The expectation is that these studies will support the fine-tuning of targets and indicators.

The gender action plan includes the development of country-, sector- and site-specific gender assessments and further stakeholder consultations (including with women and women’s groups) to better understand opportunities, barriers and challenges for women, engage marginalized groups, collect baseline data and refine targets for the gender action plan. The AE will submit a revised action plan before the end of the first year of the program.

53. The programme aims to build the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate risks in Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal. The programme intends to do so by: (i) providing support to selected LNABs to create green credit lines to finance green business projects from smallholder farmers’ organizations, cooperatives, women and youth-led organizations and MSMEs; (ii) addressing capacity-building, knowledge and policy gaps that hinder the transfer and adoption of adaptation and mitigation practices in green agricultural projects; and (iii) enhancing the collective impact of all GCF-funded projects, by establishing a coordination framework for knowledge generation and sharing, implementation and reporting.  

The AE seeks EUR 74 million in loans and EUR 30.4 million in technical assistance grants from the GCF. Additional co-financing is to be mobilized from IFAD (EUR37.7 million), the Islamic Development Bank (EUR 12.3 million) and the African Development Bank (EUR 14.8 million).  

### 4.3.2. Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)  

54. The AE, IFAD, has close to 40 years of experience in providing loans and grants to support governments in agriculture. IFAD will create a RCU that will oversee, compile, consolidate and control the work of the EEs. IFAD already has a presence in the region. Given the number of EEs involved in the programme, the AE will have an important coordinating role to ensure coherence among the activities.  

55. The EEs for the reimbursable funds are Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal, represented by their respective ministries of economy and finance. The EEs will be recipients of the GCF financing and guarantee the repayment of the GCF loan portion. The AE notes that the EEs have a track record in similar projects and have been working with the AE and other financial institutions on rural finance. The funds will be implemented by the LNABs. # GCF/B.31/02/Add.02  
## Page 163  

61. **Currency risk:** GCF will provide the loans and grants in EUR. End beneficiaries are likely disbursed in local currency. For Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Mali and Senegal, which are part of the West African Economic and Monetary Union and use the Central African franc (FCFA) as their currency, the short-term currency risk is limited because the FCFA is pegged to the Euro. However, over the long term a change in the peg could incur currency risk for the governments of these countries and affect the countries’ ability to repay the loans. The currency of Ghana, the Ghanaian cedis, is not pegged to the Euro and therefore is also exposed to currency risk in the short term. Fluctuations in the currency could impact Ghana’s ability to repay reimbursable funds. The AE notes that managing the foreign exchange risk is included as part of capacity-building activities.  

62. **Project viability and concessionality:** GCF will provide both grants and low-concessionary loans. The term sheet requires that the EEs be contractually obliged to ensure that concessionality is passed down to the final downstream recipients/borrowers. The interest rates for the sub-loans as presented in the Term Sheet are based on a near 1:1 co-financing ratio by the LNABs while co-financing requirements are 80:20 in the Term Sheet. It is advised that the margin the LNABs can charge be limited and that end-user pricing takes into account realized co-financing.

# GCF/B.31/02/Add.02  
## Page 170  

### Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of  
#### FP183  

##### Proposal name: Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN I): Greening Agricultural Banks & the Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Great Green Wall (GGW) Countries – Phase I  

##### Accredited entity: International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)  
###### Country/(ies): IGREENFIN I [Components 1 and 2]: Burkina Faso, Côte d’Ivoire, Ghana, Mali and Senegal  
###### GCF GGW Regional Support Programme [Component 3]: Burkina Faso, Chad, Djibouti, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, Senegal and Sudan  

##### Programme size: Medium  

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  

#### 1.1 Impact potential  
##### Scale: High  

1. This funding proposal is for a medium-sized, cross-cutting programme in environmental and social safeguards (ESS) Category B. The programme is submitted by 13 African States, 10 of which have least developed country (LDC) status. Five states will participate in Phase I of the IGREENFIN programme – three Great Green Wall (GGW) states, Burkina Faso, Mali and Senegal, plus Ghana and Cote d’Ivoire. Burkina Faso, Mali and Senegal, plus a further eight countries (Chad, Djibouti, Eritrea, Ethiopia, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria and Sudan), will participate in a Regional Support Programme for the GCF Great Green Wall portfolio.  

2. The total cost of the funding proposal is USD 216,900,720, including a GCF loan contribution of USD 90,000,000 and a GCF grant contribution of USD 36,900,720, with the GCF funds forming 58.5 per cent of the total cost. The remaining USD 90 million, or 41.5 per cent of the total project cost, includes grant contributions for technical assistance from IFAD and the African Development Bank (AfDB) totalling USD 51 million. Concessional loan finance totalling USD 39 million, from IFAD, Islamic Development Bank and the five National Agricultural Banks of the Phase I countries, will be blended with highly concessional GCF loan funds to ‘jumpstart’ the market for loan facilities to organizations of small-scale farmers and agro-processing and renewable energy businesses that are investing in low emission, climate resilient practices.  

3. The eleven countries of the GGW, and Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana, are among the world's poorest countries and among those most vulnerable to climate change. Agriculture, livestock and forestry activities are central to their economies and more than 70 per cent of rural communities depend directly on rainfed agriculture.  

4. The region’s agriculture, forestry and other land use, and energy sectors are responsible for just under a quarter of anthropogenic greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, generated mainly by deforestation (wood and clearing land for agriculture), livestock raising, soil and nutrient management and unsustainable rice production.  

5. Over recent decades, increasing annual average temperatures have combined with increasingly unpredictable rainfall patterns to put significant stress on production of staple and

# GCF/B.31/02/Add.02  
## Page 171  

Other crops, and some parts of the region have experienced more frequent droughts and/or floods. Climate projections indicate that precipitation levels will increase in some areas, and decrease in others, with increasingly erratic timing of growing seasons, while temperatures are expected to increase between 1 and 1.72°C for the 2031–2050 period compared to the 1986–2005 reference period. These changes, combined with direct human pressures from unsustainable land management practices and, in the absence of adaptation interventions, are expected to cause a decline in agricultural productivity, reducing food availability and farmers' incomes and fuelling competition over diminishing natural capital, greater instability, conflict and migration, especially of rural youth.

3. The overall objective of the proposed GCF programme is to create an enabling environment to increase the flow of funds to green businesses engaging in climate resilient, low emission agricultural practices in the GGW area and Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana. IGREENFIN I (Components 1 and 2) aims to build the resilience of smallholder farmers to current and future climate risks in the five target countries by: (i) providing support to selected local national agricultural banks (LNABs) for the creation of ‘green credit lines’ to finance the business projects of smallholder farmers organized in farmer organizations, cooperatives, women and youth-led organizations, and micro, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs) that will either result in lower emissions, or help adapt to changing climatic conditions, or both – such as solar photovoltaic-powered boreholes for drip irrigation; and (ii) addressing capacity-building, knowledge and policy gaps that hinder the transfer and adoption of adaptation and mitigation practices by green businesses. The Regional Support Programme (Component 3) aims to enhance the collective impact of all GCF-funded projects contributing to the GGW, including IGREENFIN I, through the establishment of a coordination framework for knowledge generation and sharing, project implementation and reporting, mobilizing private investment and promoting innovation at scale.

4. Adaptation impact: The programme will help agricultural communities in the three selected GGW countries under IGREENFIN I (Burkina Faso, Mali and Senegal) plus Côte d’Ivoire and Ghana, to reduce their vulnerability to a range of natural hazards, frequently worsened by climate change, including droughts, flooding, wildfires, water scarcity, sandstorms, extreme heat and desertification. IGREENFIN I intends to directly build the resilience and adaptive capacity of 378,600 smallholder farmers, 50 per cent female, and 50 per cent youth, indirectly benefitting the 2,494,000 people making up their households, their health and well-being, food and water security, as well as the health of the ecosystems on which their livelihoods depend. This will be achieved through the Green Business Financing Facility in the five selected countries – building the capacity of five local national agricultural banks to offer affordable financing for climate resilient, low emission agriculture interventions (green governance and lending products, green credit indicator system for management, long-term loan periods and unlocking barriers to innovative climate finance). It will support loans and technical assistance to an estimated 1,500 MSMEs and 2,500 producer organizations and cooperatives, as well as the transfer or licensing of 200 technologies and innovative solutions in agriculture and agro-processing.

5. In response to a question from the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) about the narrow focus of eligibility criteria on 10 specific value chains, the AE highlighted the benefits of focusing the banks’ loan portfolios in order to be able to provide end-to-end supply chain solutions, stating that “this approach has proven to be more effective in increasing access to financing and reducing agricultural costs and risks, than helping individual businesses in isolation”. In discussion, the AE acknowledged that additional value chains with demonstrated demand for loans and with potential for climate resilience and profitability (on a sustainable basis) could be added, following the programme’s mid-term review.

6. Mitigation impact: The cross-cutting project is expected to have a modest but significant impact in the five countries, through GHG emissions avoided as a result of the activities undertaken by the borrowing organizations using renewable energy technologies and

The major impact will come from activities undertaken in the farming/agroforestry sector, applying agroecological principles and using the farmer-managed natural regeneration and pasture restoration practices that are common in the region to switch from ongoing loss of above- and below-ground carbon through landscape degradation, to effective capture and storage of carbon at scale in healthy, productive agroecosystems. These activities, with ongoing technical assistance, are expected to result in better practices across 250,000 hectares, resulting in 4,998,515 tonnes less of CO₂eq emissions over the programme’s 25-year lifetime, compared to a scenario without the project in which climate change combines with human actions to lead to ever-worsening land degradation and desertification, with concomitant loss of above- and below-ground carbon.

## 7. The Regional Support Programme (RSP) is designed to reinforce the adaptation and mitigation benefits of IGREENFIN Phase 0 (the existing IFAD-Niger project) and Phase I, as well as 29 already approved GCF projects being implemented through 17 accredited entities (AEs) in 11 countries of the Great Green Wall.  

These countries are contributing to the GGW’s African-led initiatives to restore the productivity and vitality of the highly climate change-vulnerable Sahel region, with the goals to restore 100 million hectares of degraded land, sequester 250 million tonnes of carbon, and create 10 million green jobs by 2030.  

The RSP aims to strengthen GGW countries’ institutional capacity on reporting, generating and sharing innovative solutions and knowledge innovation on green business and accessible financing, and increase private sector financial participation in green business projects.  

The RSP has a technical capacity component that aims to enhance knowledge management and exchanges, accelerating the uptake of best practices and learning and informing policy and investments. However, the funding proposal is not explicit on the type of policy support envisaged, and detail is not provided on the concrete deliverables expected from the activities.  

## 8. One of the aims of the RSP is “to facilitate more consistent and credible reporting of climate results of GCF-funded projects/programmes, which can be aggregated and reported as part of the interventions of the GGWI”.  

The independent TAP foresees that it will be challenging to ‘retrofit’ new indicators for tracking and monitoring portfolio-wide GCF investments related to the GGW, given the diverse nature of the 29 projects already existing, and believes this process has the potential to cause confusion with the existing project-level monitoring and evaluation systems, but understands that it will be important to try and achieve this.  

## 9. The independent TAP assesses the impact potential of this cross-cutting project as high, including both mitigation and adaptation aspects.  
### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential                                                            Scale: Medium

# GCF/B.31/02/Add.02

In discussion with the AE, the independent TAP expressed the view that the programme’s capacity-building of the national agricultural banks and intermediary financial institutions, and the technical assistance to borrowers, would go some way to enabling more accurate risk assessment and eliminating misperceptions, and also enabling business success and reasonable repayment rates. Many factors would likely remain beyond the programme’s influence, however, such as underdeveloped communications and transportation infrastructure, and covariate risks due to rainfall and price fluctuations. This could mean that transaction costs and default rates remain fairly high, once the subsidizing effect of the programme’s concessional financing and technical assistance is removed, such that interest rates offered by the agricultural and other banks post-project increase again, indicating that there has not been a fundamental shift in how the sector operates.

In response, the AE highlighted that “With the IGREENFIN programme, IFAD aims to jump-start this market, which is still in the incipient phase in many countries. By building the [local national agricultural banks] LNABs’ capacity to offer affordable green finance for smallholder agriculture and the capacity of smallholders farmers organized in cooperatives, farm groups and MSMEs to design and implement bankable green projects, the programme will begin to fill the gap in financing for agriculture in the region and alter the general perception of smallholder agriculture as a risky business to one that views this sector as a vast market with great potential and opportunities. Throughout the programme’s lifecycle, studies and policy papers will be produced on climate risk management to support institutional capacity-building and the design of lending products. This will help encourage the financial sector to invest more in green businesses and reduce the cost of the risks, which is usually the motive for charging high interest rates on loans to smallholder farmers. The partnership with central banks and governments will contribute to maintaining these specific provisions for this specific targeted group after the programme ends.”

According to IFAD, the determinants of interest rates on loans include:  
(i) cost of resource,  
(ii) the management fee,  
(iii) the cost of risk, and  
(iv) the margin.  

In the targeted countries, the first three determinants are high for the simple reason that there is lack of capital. Target groups submit very few green projects for financing because of the lack of incentives and the general misperception of this group, despite the fact that they generate most of the agricultural production in their countries. This leads banks to focus on medium and big farmers only. To substantially reduce interest rates and reach smallholder farmers, IGREENFIN will support the strengthening of the banks’ know-how to raise capital on external markets and reduce the cost of resource, and to develop bankable pipelines, which will reduce the management cost for a limited number of projects, as well as the high risk perception. By responding to those determinants of the interest with the programme interventions, after the programme ends, accredited banks will build on the experience to develop this market further and continue working on green business projects and green bonds, in accordance with their vision and strategy already shared with the AE. Additionally, the target countries’ central banks will be better equipped to support green finance sector reform and policies and promote specific incentives to support green growth within the financial sector as well as financial inclusion of smallholder farmers.

The AE also pointed to “the rapid development change obtained in current or similar experiences in the region such as ‘Accueil | DER/FJ - Délégation Générale à l’Entrepreneuriat Rapide pour les Femmes et les Jeunes’ in Senegal; SUNREF program, with AfDB initiatives with MSMEs and a similar programme backed by World Bank, and the current movement ‘Home | Finance in Common’, which IFAD and partners are supporting. These initial initiatives have helped get the process started in many sectors, including in smallholder agriculture with local public agricultural banks ... Since then, they have attracted more resources and the portfolios

### 1.3 Sustainable development potential           Scale: High  

16. In addition to the planned adaptation and mitigation benefits, the farming, renewable energy and business activities to be supported through the loan portfolio of the IGREENFIN I programme (Components 1 and 2) are expected to have a number of co-benefits for sustainable development, including environmental, social and economic co-benefits, and benefits in terms of gender sensitivity and social inclusion.  

17. Environmental co-benefits: Assuming that the loan programme’s Technical Assistance Facility is successful in promoting a combination of traditional knowledge on woodland and pasture regeneration with modern best practices in agroforestry, crop and livestock farming, there is potential for multiple environmental benefits – including increased biodiversity, enhanced soil fertility and stability, maximization of soil moisture and groundwater resources, and improved storage and sequestration of carbon in regreened agroecological landscapes.  

18. Insofar as the Regional Support Programme will help to maximize the collective impact of the GCF Great Green Wall portfolio of 29 existing projects, there is potential for significant global environmental benefits in terms of the GGW’s overall objectives of combating desertification. Scientists have observed an expansion of the Sahara Desert by 10 per cent over the past century, affecting regional food and water security, and also influencing global weather patterns and human health, as huge seasonal dust clouds are carried across the Atlantic as far as Central America. The ambitious Great Green Wall concept represents humanity’s collective effort to stem the encroachment of the desert into the Sahel by supporting sustainable livelihoods on restored and regreened land that also reduces atmospheric emissions.  

19. Building on the brief references in the funding proposal to the five pillars of the Great Green Wall Accelerator, including Pillar 2 on “Sustainable management of ecosystems and land restoration”, the IGREENFIN I programme’s specific approaches to promoting regenerative, restorative and sustainable approaches for management of cattle and grazing lands, and for agroforestry, have been outlined in detailed response documents from the AE.  

20. The independent TAP would like to put on record two risks associated with loans through the IGREENFIN programme for production of commodity tree crops: the risk that loans could be used for monocrop plantations without the environmental benefits of mixed-use agroforestry, and the risk that areas of intact or partial indigenous woodland could be cleared for tree-crop production, contributing to deforestation. Table 16 in the funding proposal, “Preliminary selected sustainable value chains”, highlights the benefits of mixed agroforestry – growing indigenous tree and other species in combination with the focus commodity tree crops of mango, cashew and shea. The independent TAP notes, however, that use of other species is not mentioned by the example loan applicants in the indicative loan pipeline in annex 23, who reference only the cultivation of the commodity crops.
